```json
      "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\nWe study lead-lag analysis pitfalls in iterative training collapse using Pythia-410M \\cite{biderman2023pythia}, a 410M-parameter decoder-only language model.\nPythia provides a controlled setting: small enough for multi-seed experiments across five contamination levels, yet large enough to exhibit meaningful generation-over-generation degradation.\nCross-architecture validation uses GPT-2-medium (345M parameters, also decoder-only), a second transformer family of similar scale; results are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:method_sensitivity}, \\S\\ref{sec:regime}, and \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}.\nTraining data is drawn from WikiText-103 \\cite{merity2017pointer}, using 5{,}000-sample subsets per generation.\n\nOur protocol follows the replace paradigm of \\citet{shumailov2024curse}, adapted for mixed synthetic-real training at $\\alpha{<}1.0$.\nEach generation $t$ proceeds in three steps: (1)~fine-tune the generation-$(t{-}1)$ model for 5{,}000 steps on a mixture of $\\alpha$ fraction synthetic data and $(1{-}\\alpha)$ fraction real data (AdamW optimizer, learning rate $5 \\times 10^{-5}$, 500 warmup steps, weight decay $0.01$, batch size 4 for Pythia-410M, 32 for GPT-2-medium); (2)~generate 5{,}000 synthetic samples from the fine-tuned model (temperature $1.0$, top-$k{=}50$, max length 512 tokens); (3)~fully replace the synthetic pool.\nWe run $T{=}10$ generations (11 time points including the generation-0 baseline) across contamination levels $\\alpha \\in \\{0.00, 0.25, 0.50, 0.75, 1.00\\}$.\nThe $\\alpha{=}1.0$ condition uses four random seeds $\\{42, 43, 44, 45\\}$; $\\alpha{=}0.00$, $\\alpha{=}0.25$, and $\\alpha{=}0.75$ each use three seeds $\\{42, 43, 44\\}$; $\\alpha{=}0.50$ uses three seeds $\\{42, 43, 44\\}$.\n\nWe track two \\emph{canary metrics}---cheap-to-compute statistics hypothesized to precede downstream degradation, inspired by early-warning signals in dynamical systems \\cite{scheffer2009early}---and three \\emph{downstream metrics}.\nCanary metrics are token entropy (next-token prediction entropy averaged over held-out samples) and expected calibration error (ECE).\nDownstream metrics are distinct-1 and distinct-2 ($n$-gram diversity \\cite{li2016diversity}, computed over all 5{,}000 generated samples per generation) and perplexity (evaluated on held-out real data).\nToken entropy is the mean next-token prediction entropy $H(p_\\theta | \\text{context})$ averaged over 1{,}000 held-out samples; ECE uses 15 equal-width bins.\nWe additionally evaluate effective rank and Self-BLEU as candidate canaries (\\S\\ref{sec:case_study}).\nResults for distinct-3 are reported in Appendix~\\ref{app:distinct3}.\nCode and data will be released upon publication.\n\nAll hypothesis tests apply Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) FDR correction at $q{=}0.05$.\nPermutation Granger causality tests use 5{,}000 permutations on first-differenced series \\cite{shojaie2022granger}.\nCross-correlation analysis uses a maximum lag of 5 generations.\nTable~\\ref{tab:drift_summary} summarizes raw metric drift from generation~0 to generation~10 across all five contamination levels.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Metric drift (\\%, generation~0$\\to$10) for Pythia-410M across contamination levels. Diversity collapse occurs at all $\\alpha$ levels, with the strongest decline at moderate contamination ($\\alpha{=}0.25$: distinct-1 $-40.3{\\pm}0.3\\%$) rather than full replacement ($\\alpha{=}1.0$: $-27.8{\\pm}1.6\\%$). $\\alpha{=}0.00$, $\\alpha{=}0.25$, $\\alpha{=}0.75$: 3-seed mean${\\pm}$std (seeds 42, 43, 44); $\\alpha{=}1.0$: 4-seed mean${\\pm}$std (seeds 42, 43, 44, 45); $\\alpha{=}0.50$: seed~42.}\n\\label{tab:drift_summary}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\alpha$ & Entropy & ECE & Distinct-1 & Distinct-2 & Perplexity \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.00 & $-4.6{\\pm}0.4$ & $+37.6{\\pm}14$ & $-14.1{\\pm}0.4$ & $-9.6{\\pm}0.4$ & $-20.6{\\pm}1.0$ \\\\\n0.25 & $-4.7{\\pm}0.5$ & $+81.8{\\pm}15.9$ & $-40.3{\\pm}0.3$ & $-56.4{\\pm}0.7$ & $-18.1{\\pm}0.8$ \\\\\n0.50 & $-18.6$ & $+842.8$ & $-37.3$ & $-51.5$ & $+21.1$ \\\\\n0.75 & $-27.6{\\pm}0.2$ & $+1791{\\pm}18$ & $-32.3{\\pm}0.5$ & $-41.0{\\pm}1.0$ & $+182.6{\\pm}0.7$ \\\\\n1.00 & $-60.8{\\pm}1.2$ & $+3780{\\pm}30$ & $-27.8{\\pm}1.6$ & $-34.0{\\pm}3.3$ & $+3674{\\pm}45$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Method Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{sec:method_sensitivity}\n\nWe open with Claim~3---method sensitivity---because it conditions the interpretation of all subsequent results.\nIf different statistical methods yield contradictory conclusions on the same data, any single-method analysis is unreliable by construction.\n\n\\paragraph{Five-method ablation.}\nWe apply five lead-lag detection methods to the $\\alpha{=}1.0$ condition (4~seeds, 16 canary$\\to$downstream pairs).\nThe fraction of pairs reaching BH-corrected FDR significance varies dramatically by method: naive cross-correlation \\textbf{16/16} (100\\%), differenced cross-correlation \\textbf{14/16} (87.5\\%), threshold onset detection \\textbf{9/16} (56.3\\%), permutation Granger causality \\textbf{6/16} (37.5\\%), and Toda--Yamamoto Granger \\textbf{4/16} (25.0\\%).\nMethod choice alone swings significance from \\textbf{100\\% to 25\\%}: 50--57\\% of pairs classified as significant under one method flip to non-significant under another.\nThe spurious-signal rate---the fraction of pairs detected by the most liberal method but not confirmed by the most conservative---is \\textbf{75\\%} across all five methods and \\textbf{62.5\\%} when excluding Toda--Yamamoto, whose low statistical power at $T{=}10$ inflates this rate (\\S\\ref{sec:consensus_validation}).\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} reports significance rates restricted to 8 validated canary$\\to$downstream pairs (excluding failed candidates); the 16-pair rates above include effective rank and Self-BLEU to capture the full method-sensitivity landscape.\n\n\\paragraph{First-differencing diagnostic.}\nA single preprocessing decision can eliminate an entire analysis channel.\nFor canary$\\to$diversity pairs at $\\alpha{=}1.0$, all pairs retain unanimous FDR significance after first-differencing (24/24$\\to$24/24), confirming genuine temporal precedence rather than shared-trend confounding.\nFor canary$\\to$perplexity, however, undifferenced tests find near-universal significance ($F{=}16$--$36$) while differenced tests find \\textbf{zero} significant pairs ($F{\\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$; Figure~\\ref{fig:differencing_dmax}a).\nThe apparent canary$\\to$perplexity relationship is entirely an artifact of co-trending.\nNotably, at $\\alpha{=}0.75$, differenced cross-correlation finds 8/8 pairs significant in \\emph{both} forward and reverse directions per seed, demonstrating that this method alone cannot distinguish genuine directional precedence from shared dynamics even after detrending.\n\n\\paragraph{Augmentation order sensitivity.}\nToda--Yamamoto Granger tests show extreme sensitivity to the augmentation order $d_{\\max}$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:differencing_dmax}b).\nAt $d_{\\max}{=}0$ (most permissive), approximately 6\\% of pairs are rejected; at adaptive $d_{\\max}$ determined by ADF unit-root tests, 75\\%; at $d_{\\max}{=}1$ (most conservative), \\textbf{94\\%}.\nThis single hyperparameter swings conclusions from near-universal significance to near-universal non-significance.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{figures/differencing_dmax.pdf}\n\\caption{First-differencing diagnostic and augmentation order sensitivity (Pythia-410M, $\\alpha{=}1.0$, 4~seeds). \\textbf{(a)}~First-differencing eliminates the canary$\\to$perplexity channel: undifferenced tests find near-universal significance ($F{=}16$--$36$) while differenced tests find zero significant pairs ($F{\\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$), exposing these associations as shared-trend artifacts. Canary$\\to$diversity pairs retain significance after differencing (24/24$\\to$24/24). \\textbf{(b)}~Toda--Yamamoto Granger significance rates vary dramatically with augmentation order $d_{\\max}$: from $\\sim$6\\% rejection at $d_{\\max}{=}0$ to 94\\% at $d_{\\max}{=}1$, demonstrating that this single hyperparameter can reverse conclusions about temporal precedence.}\n\\label{fig:differencing_dmax}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Inter-method agreement.}\nCohen's $\\kappa$, averaged over all method pairs, is \\textbf{0.185} (near chance; poor agreement); this value is consistent across contamination levels ($\\alpha{=}0.0$ through $\\alpha{=}1.0$), indicating that method disagreement is a structural property of the analysis rather than a regime-specific artifact.\nCross-architecture validation on GPT-2-medium (12 canary$\\to$downstream pairs) yields Fleiss' $\\kappa{=}0.091$, also in the ``slight agreement'' range, confirming that low inter-method agreement is not architecture-specific.\nRestricting to three informative methods (excluding naive cross-correlation and Toda--Yamamoto, which anchor the permissive and conservative extremes respectively) raises $\\kappa$ to 0.667 (substantial agreement), indicating that the low full-panel $\\kappa$ is driven by the deliberate inclusion of methods spanning the entire permissive-to-conservative spectrum rather than by fundamental incoherence among methods.\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} extends the analysis across contamination levels.\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (no synthetic data, serving as null control), naive cross-correlation finds \\textbf{100\\%} of pairs significant---a textbook spurious-regression outcome in trending time series.\nPermutation Granger correctly finds 0\\% across all three null-control seeds; Toda--Yamamoto finds 0--12.5\\% (1/24 total, attributable to a degenerate $\\text{df}_{\\text{resid}}{=}1$ case; \\S\\ref{sec:consensus_validation}).\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.50$, correlation-based methods (naive, differenced, threshold) unanimously find significance while causal methods unanimously find non-significance (Figure~\\ref{fig:consensus_heatmap}), the most extreme method$\\times$regime divergence in our experiments.\n\n\\paragraph{Method$\\times$regime interaction.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} reveals that the correlation-versus-causal divergence is not uniform across contamination levels.\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.25$, differenced cross-correlation drops to 25\\% and threshold onset to 37.5\\%, while naive cross-correlation remains at 100\\%---demonstrating that even partial detrending removes marginal associations at low contamination.\nIn the lowest-contamination regime ($\\alpha{\\leq}0.25$, three seeds), the false confirmation rate for the most liberal method approaches 100\\%, while all Granger-type tests correctly find 0\\%---a regime where single-method analysis is maximally dangerous.\nThe graded response across methods, combined with the block structure visible in Figure~\\ref{fig:consensus_heatmap}, motivates the regime-dependent analysis in \\S\\ref{sec:regime}.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{figures/consensus_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Method$\\times$regime consensus heatmap (Pythia-410M). Each cell shows the consensus tier ($K$-of-$M$ agreement) for a given lead-lag method (rows) and contamination level $\\alpha$ (columns). A clear block structure emerges: correlation-based methods (naive, differenced, threshold) find significance across all $\\alpha$ levels, while Granger-type methods find significance only at $\\alpha{\\geq}0.75$. The $\\alpha{=}0.50$ column is maximally dangerous---high correlation coexists with zero causal precedence---illustrating Pitfall~3 (regime dependence). Color scale: dark = significant (Tier~3), light = non-significant (Tier~1).}\n\\label{fig:consensus_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{FDR significance rates (\\%) for five lead-lag methods across contamination levels (Pythia-410M). Method choice swings significance from 100\\% (naive cross-correlation) to 0\\% (permutation Granger) at $\\alpha{\\leq}0.25$, with seed-dependent onset at $\\alpha{=}0.50$, demonstrating that single-method conclusions are unreliable. Each cell reports the fraction of 8 canary$\\to$downstream pairs (token entropy and ECE $\\times$ 4 downstream metrics) reaching BH-corrected significance ($q{=}0.05$), excluding failed candidates (\\S\\ref{sec:case_study}). Seed coverage: $\\alpha{=}1.0$: 4 seeds (32 pairs); $\\alpha{=}0.00$, $\\alpha{=}0.75$: 3 seeds (24 pairs); $\\alpha{=}0.25$ Toda--Yamamoto: 3 seeds (24 pairs), other methods single seed; $\\alpha{=}0.50$: 3~seeds for permutation Granger (range shown), single seed for other methods. $^\\dagger$Seed-dependent onset: s42${=}$0/8, s43${=}$6/8, s44${=}$3/8 (median 3/8).}\n\\label{tab:method_comparison}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & $\\alpha{=}0.0$ & $\\alpha{=}0.25$ & $\\alpha{=}0.50$ & $\\alpha{=}0.75$ & $\\alpha{=}1.0$ & FPR$_\\text{null}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nNaive XCorr     & 100   & 100  & 100  & 100   & 100   & 100  \\\\\nDiff.\\ XCorr   & 37.5  & 25   & 100  & 100   & 100   & 37.5 \\\\\nThreshold       & 62.5  & 37.5 & 100  & 50    & 100   & 62.5 \\\\\nPerm.\\ Granger  & 0     & 0    & 0--75$^\\dagger$  & 79.2  & 75    & 0    \\\\\nToda--Yamamoto  & 0     & 12.5 & 0    & 12.5  & 59.4  & 0    \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Regime-Dependent Detectability}\n\\label{sec:regime}\n\nBeyond method choice, the contamination level~$\\alpha$ determines whether a temporal lead exists to detect.\nWe present regime effects at two levels: raw metric drift (method-independent) and Granger-precedence detectability.\n\n\\paragraph{Raw drift.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:drift_summary} shows that diversity collapse occurs at \\emph{all} contamination levels, not only under full synthetic replacement.\nDistinct-1 declines by 13.8\\% even at $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (attributable to fine-tuning on repeated real-data subsets), and more steeply at intermediate contamination: $-40.3{\\pm}0.3\\%$ ($\\alpha{=}0.25$), $-37.3\\%$ ($\\alpha{=}0.50$), $-32.3{\\pm}0.5\\%$ ($\\alpha{=}0.75$), and $-27.8{\\pm}1.6\\%$ ($\\alpha{=}1.0$).\nThe non-monotonic pattern---diversity declining \\emph{more} at moderate~$\\alpha$ than at extreme~$\\alpha$---is confirmed by 3-seed replication at $\\alpha{=}0.25$ (std${=}0.3\\%$), indicating that this relationship between contamination level and collapse severity is robust rather than a seed-specific artifact.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{figures/regime_map.pdf}\n\\caption{Detectability regime map across contamination levels (Pythia-410M). Permutation Granger FDR significance rate (solid line) and consensus tier (shaded regions) as a function of $\\alpha$. The onset boundary lies at $\\alpha{\\approx}0.25$--$0.50$: at $\\alpha{\\leq}0.25$, all seeds agree on null (0/24); at $\\alpha{=}0.50$, detectability is seed-dependent (0/8--6/8, 3~seeds, median 3/8); at $\\alpha{=}0.75$, directional asymmetry emerges robustly (19/24 forward, 0/24 reverse, 3~seeds) but method consensus remains transitional (Tier~2--3); at $\\alpha{=}1.0$, full Confirmatory consensus is achieved (24/24 forward, Tier~3, 4~seeds). Multi-seed replication: $\\alpha{=}0.00$, $\\alpha{=}0.25$, $\\alpha{=}0.50$, $\\alpha{=}0.75$: 3~seeds; $\\alpha{=}1.0$: 4~seeds.}\n\\label{fig:regime_map}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Granger-precedence detectability.}\nDespite ubiquitous drift, causal detectability is sharply regime-dependent (Figure~\\ref{fig:regime_map}).\nPermutation Granger tests (first-differenced, BH-corrected) find 0/24 pairs FDR-significant at $\\alpha{=}0.00$ (3~seeds), 0/24 at $\\alpha{=}0.25$ (3~seeds), 0/8--6/8 at $\\alpha{=}0.50$ (seed-dependent; 3~seeds, median 3/8), and \\textbf{6/8} at $\\alpha{=}1.0$.\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.75$ (3~seeds), permutation Granger finds 19/24 forward-direction pairs FDR-significant with 0/24 reverse, confirming directional asymmetry; however, method-level consensus varies by seed (Tier~3 pairs: 4, 3, and 1 across seeds), placing this regime in a transitional detection state.\nUnder Bonferroni correction, approximately 3/8 pairs per seed survive---consistent with the transitional nature of the $\\alpha{=}0.75$ boundary where marginal signals are sensitive to correction stringency.\nWithin-$\\alpha$ multi-seed analysis at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ yields \\textbf{24/24} canary$\\to$diversity pairs FDR-significant across all four seeds, with 0/24 in the reverse direction, confirming temporal asymmetry.\nThe resulting dose-response pattern is monotonic: causal detectability is absent below $\\alpha{=}0.25$, seed-dependent at $\\alpha{=}0.50$, and robust above $\\alpha{=}0.75$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:regime_map}).\nThe onset boundary lies at $\\alpha{\\approx}0.25$--$0.50$, consistent with theoretical expectations about signal-to-noise limits in short time series~\\cite{boettiger2012limits}.\nAt the extremes, the transition is unambiguous: 0/24 FDR-significant pairs at $\\alpha{\\leq}0.25$ (3~seeds) versus 24/24 at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (4~seeds, Tier~3 consensus).\nBetween these extremes, individual-seed stochasticity determines whether the consensus threshold is crossed; the data map a boundary region rather than a sharp phase transition.\nSensitivity analysis confirms that treatment/null separation is robust to FDR threshold choice ($q{=}0.01$: 0/24 null, 0/24 treatment at $\\alpha{=}0.75$; $q{=}0.10$: 0/24 null, 24/24 treatment; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}).\nCross-architecture validation on GPT-2-medium confirms this boundary: at $\\alpha{=}0.25$, at most 1/8 pairs reach Confirmatory consensus ($K{\\geq}4/5$)---consistent with the null regime---despite substantially stronger canary drift (entropy $-32.8\\%$ vs.\\ Pythia $-4.7{\\pm}0.5\\%$), while at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ all 6 canary$\\to$diversity pairs reach $K{\\geq}4/5$ (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_arch_regime} summarizes the regime map across both architectures.\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.25$, GPT-2-medium yields 1/8 pairs at Tier~3 ($K{\\geq}4/5$) and 7/8 at Tier~2 ($K{\\geq}3/5$)---consistent with Pythia's 0/8 Tier~3, 0/8 Tier~2 result at the same contamination level, placing both architectures in the null/low-detectability regime.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-architecture regime map: lead-lag consensus tier and metric drift at $\\alpha{=}0.25$ and $\\alpha{=}1.0$. Tier~3: $K{\\geq}4/5$ methods; Tier~2: $K{\\geq}3/5$. Drift is generation~0$\\to$10. GPT-2-medium $\\alpha{=}0.25$: single seed (seed=42); Pythia $\\alpha{=}0.25$: 3-seed mean${\\pm}$std. The onset boundary ($\\alpha{\\approx}0.25$--$0.50$) is consistent across two transformer decoder-only model families of similar scale.}\n\\label{tab:cross_arch_regime}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\alpha$ & Model & Entropy & ECE & Distinct-1 & Distinct-2 & Perm.\\ Granger & Tier~3 \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.25 & Pythia-410M  & $-4.7{\\pm}0.5$ & $+81.8{\\pm}15.9$ & $-40.3{\\pm}0.3$ & $-56.4{\\pm}0.7$ & 0/8 & 0/8 \\\\\n0.25 & GPT-2-medium & $-32.8$ & $+422$ & $-23.5$ & $-31.6$ & 0/8 & 1/8 \\\\\n\\midrule\n1.00 & Pythia-410M  & $-60.8{\\pm}1.2$ & $+3780{\\pm}30$ & $-27.8{\\pm}1.6$ & $-34.0{\\pm}3.3$ & 6/8 & 6/6 \\\\\n1.00 & GPT-2-medium & \\multicolumn{6}{c}{(reported in \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}; all 6 canary$\\to$diversity Tier~3)} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Onset boundary at $\\alpha{=}0.50$.}\nThe $\\alpha{=}0.50$ regime provides particularly informative evidence.\nCorrelation-based methods unanimously find significance (Table~\\ref{tab:method_comparison}), and causal methods confirm emerging detectability in a majority of seeds: two of three seeds show directional asymmetry under permutation Granger (6/8 and 3/8 forward, 0/8 reverse), while one seed shows synchronous collapse (0/8).\nThe majority-signal outcome places $\\alpha{=}0.50$ in a transitional regime where causal detectability is emerging but not yet robust, bridging the null regime ($\\alpha{\\leq}0.25$) and the consistently detectable regime ($\\alpha{\\geq}0.75$).\nThis seed dependence exemplifies our central argument: even within a single regime and method, stochastic variation in training determines whether the consensus threshold is crossed.\nAt this boundary, canary sensitivity is metric-dependent: token entropy fails to reach FDR significance in any seed ($p_{\\text{fdr}}{\\geq}0.086$), while ECE achieves significance in the two signal-positive seeds (s43, s44), identifying ECE as the more sensitive canary at the onset boundary.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:consensus_heatmap} visualizes this interaction across all method$\\times\\alpha$ combinations, revealing a clear block structure that separates correlation-based methods from causal methods as a function of regime.\n\n\n\\subsection{Case Study: Canary Prediction of Diversity Collapse}\n\\label{sec:case_study}\n\nHaving established method sensitivity (Claim~3) and regime dependence (Claim~2), we present the conditions under which canary prediction succeeds (Claim~1).\nWe scope this claim precisely: canary metrics predict \\emph{diversity collapse} (declining distinct-$n$), not model collapse broadly.\n\n\\paragraph{Full synthetic replacement ($\\alpha{=}1.0$, 4 seeds).}\nToken entropy exhibits robust Granger-precedence over diversity collapse metrics, confirmed by 4 independent statistical methods (permutation Granger power${=}1.0$), 4 seeds, and cross-architecture replication on GPT-2-medium (Tier~3 consensus; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nCross-correlation peaks at lag$={+}1$ with $|r|{>}0.99$ (cross-seed standard deviation: 0.0), establishing a consistent one-generation lead.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:metric_trajectory} illustrates the one-generation offset between token entropy decline and distinct-1 decline, visible across all four seeds.\nPermutation Granger tests (first-differenced) confirm all canary$\\to$diversity pairs as FDR-significant across all four seeds, including distinct-3 (Appendix~\\ref{app:distinct3}).\nReverse-direction tests (downstream$\\to$canary) find \\textbf{0/24} FDR-significant (all $p{>}0.49$), establishing complete directional asymmetry.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{figures/metric_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Metric trajectories across 10 generations at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (Pythia-410M, 4~seeds shown as individual traces). Token entropy (top, blue) drops sharply at generation~1, while distinct-1 (bottom, red) begins declining at generations~2--3, establishing a visible one-generation canary lead. The consistent lag across all four seeds confirms that the temporal offset is robust rather than seed-specific. Shaded bands show the inter-seed range. This visual pattern corresponds to the statistical finding of 24/24 forward-direction and 0/24 reverse-direction FDR-significant pairs.}\n\\label{fig:metric_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{High contamination ($\\alpha{=}0.75$, 3 seeds).}\nDirectional asymmetry is robustly confirmed across three independent seeds (permutation Granger: 19/24 forward pairs FDR-significant, 0/24 reverse).\nHowever, method-level consensus varies by seed (Tier~3 pairs: 4, 3, and 1 across seeds 42, 43, 44), placing $\\alpha{=}0.75$ squarely in the transitional detection regime.\nDifferenced cross-correlation finds 8/8 pairs significant in \\emph{both} forward and reverse directions at each seed, confirming that this method alone cannot distinguish genuine directional precedence from shared dynamics---a seed-level demonstration of Pitfall~1 (\\S\\ref{sec:pitfalls}).\n\n\\paragraph{ECE as a conditional secondary canary.}\nECE performs equivalently to token entropy across four of five methods (naive, differenced, threshold, permutation Granger).\nIts classification as ``secondary'' is driven entirely by Toda--Yamamoto Type~II errors at $T{=}10$, where bootstrap power analysis yields estimated power of 0.05--0.20 depending on $d_{\\max}$.\nResolving ECE's true canary status requires longer time series.\n\n\\paragraph{Failed canary candidates.}\nNot all metrics serve as canaries.\nEffective rank and Self-BLEU show no detectable lead-lag relationship with any downstream metric under any method or contamination level, and are excluded from the consensus framework.\nEffective rank behavior is model-dependent in an unexpected way: in Pythia-410M, effective rank decreases during collapse (representational contraction), whereas in GPT-2-medium at $\\alpha{=}0.25$ it \\emph{increases} from 2.37 to 8.48 (+258\\%).\nThe GPT-2-medium starting effective rank is near rank-2, suggesting the model's initial representation is already highly concentrated; iterative training disperses it rather than concentrating it further.\nThis opposite directionality across architectures precludes effective rank as a universal canary and motivates architecture-specific analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\paragraph{Consensus filtering.}\nOf 16 canary$\\to$downstream pairs tested at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (4 candidate canaries $\\times$ 4 downstream metrics), \\textbf{6 survive} the full consensus pipeline ($K{\\geq}4/5$ methods, $S{\\geq}3/4$ seeds): token entropy and ECE each predicting distinct-1 and distinct-2 (4 pairs; distinct-3 results in Appendix~\\ref{app:distinct3} bring the total to~6).\nThe 10 eliminated pairs comprise all canary$\\to$perplexity relationships (identified as spurious by the first-differencing diagnostic) and all effective-rank and Self-BLEU pairs (failed canaries).\nThe consensus framework reduces the naive 100\\% significance rate to the 37.5\\% rate supported by the most conservative individual methods (Figure~\\ref{fig:consensus_workflow}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Consensus Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:consensus_validation}\n\nWe validate the consensus framework's calibration using built-in experimental controls.\n\n\\paragraph{False positive rate.}\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (three seeds, 24 total pairs), no synthetic data is injected; any detected lead-lag association is a false positive.\nIndividual-method FPR ranges from \\textbf{100\\%} (naive cross-correlation) through 62.5\\% (threshold) and 37.5\\% (differenced) down to \\textbf{0\\%} (permutation Granger: 0/24 across all three seeds).\nThe consensus framework at $K{\\geq}4/5$ achieves \\textbf{FPR${=}$0\\%} (0/24 pairs, three independent seeds), matching the best individual methods while providing robustness against miscalibration of any single method (Table~\\ref{tab:fpr_fnr}).\n\n\\paragraph{False negative rate.}\nAt $\\alpha{=}1.0$, we define 6 effect-size reference pairs (token entropy and ECE $\\to$ distinct-1/2/3) using criteria independent of the consensus methods: drift ratio ${\\geq}3\\times$ the $\\alpha{=}0.0$ baseline (entropy $11.93\\times$, ECE $73.12\\times$) and canary onset strictly preceding downstream onset by ${\\geq}1$ generation (\\S\\ref{sec:calibration}).\nFNR is calibrated on a 4-method panel (excluding differenced cross-correlation to avoid circularity): at $K{\\geq}3/4$, $S{\\geq}3/4$, all~6 are recovered (\\textbf{FNR${=}$0\\%}).\nToda--Yamamoto alone yields FNR${=}$33.3\\% (2/6), missing ECE$\\to$distinct-2 and ECE$\\to$distinct-3 due to Type~II errors at $T{=}10$.\nBoth FPR and FNR are validated on the joint method${\\times}$seed dimension (FPR: 3~seeds at $\\alpha{=}0.0$; FNR: 4~seeds at $\\alpha{=}1.0$), with cross-seed FPR variance negligible (all three seeds yield 0/8 robust pairs at $K{\\geq}4/5$).\nTable~\\ref{tab:fpr_fnr} reports the calibration results.\n\n\\paragraph{Bootstrap power analysis.}\nTo quantify detection power beyond binary FNR, we perform bootstrap power analysis ($B{=}1{,}000$ resamples from 4-seed data at $\\alpha{=}1.0$).\nPermutation Granger achieves power${=}1.0$ on genuine pairs (canary$\\to$diversity) and power${=}0.0$ on spurious pairs (canary$\\to$perplexity), providing clean separation.\nToda--Yamamoto achieves power${=}0.05$--$0.20$ on genuine pairs (depending on $d_{\\max}$), confirming its poor sensitivity at $T{=}10$.\nWe note that bootstrap power${=}1.0$ reflects the effect size in our 4-seed data and should not be interpreted as a universal power guarantee; furthermore, the two methods use different data-generating processes for calibration (permutation Granger resamples observed $\\alpha{=}1.0$ trajectories, while Toda--Yamamoto power was estimated under a generic VAR(2) simulation with $\\beta{=}0.3$).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{False positive and false negative rates for individual methods and the consensus framework (Pythia-410M). The consensus threshold $K{\\geq}4/5$ achieves FPR${=}$0\\% and FNR${=}$0\\%, matching the best individual method (permutation Granger) while providing robustness against miscalibration. FPR: $\\alpha{=}0.0$ null control (three seeds, $M{=}5$ methods); values are 3-seed means with ranges in parentheses where cross-seed variance is non-negligible. FNR: $\\alpha{=}1.0$ against 6 effect-size reference pairs (4-method panel excluding differenced cross-correlation, 4~seeds).}\n\\label{tab:fpr_fnr}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nCriterion & FPR ($\\alpha{=}0.0$) & FNR ($\\alpha{=}1.0$) \\\\\n\\midrule\nNaive XCorr              & 100\\%              & 0\\%  \\\\\nDifferenced XCorr        & 37.5\\%             & 0\\%  \\\\\nThreshold Onset          & 29.2\\% (12.5--62.5) & 0\\%  \\\\\nPermutation Granger      & 0\\%                & 0\\%  \\\\\nToda--Yamamoto           & 4.2\\% (0--12.5)    & 33.3\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nConsensus ($K{\\geq}4/5$)              & 0\\%    & 0\\%  \\\\\nConsensus ($K{\\geq}4/5$, $S{\\geq}3/4$) & 0\\%    & 0\\%  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}",
```

```text
1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Granger precedence is not causation.}
4	Granger causality tests whether past values of one series improve prediction of another beyond its own history; it does not establish true causation~\cite{granger1969investigating,shojaie2022granger}.
5	Bivariate Granger tests cannot exclude confounders: an unobserved process---such as a latent distributional shift in the training data---could independently drive both token entropy and diversity metrics, producing spurious lead-lag associations even when no direct relationship exists.
6	Our multi-method consensus framework inherits this limitation in full.
7	Throughout this paper, ``Granger precedence'' denotes predictive improvement in a time-series forecasting sense, not a causal mechanism.
8	This distinction is why we scope our central claim to ``token entropy Granger-precedes diversity collapse'' rather than asserting that token entropy \emph{causes} diversity collapse.
9	
10	\paragraph{Calibration scope and generalization.}
11	All FPR and FNR calibration results reported in this work are specific to our experimental setup: Pythia-410M~\cite{biderman2023pythia}, a WikiText-103 subset, $T{=}10$ generation cycles, and five contamination levels ($\alpha \in \{0.0, 0.25, 0.50, 0.75, 1.0\}$).
12	Generalization to other model architectures, datasets, or training protocols requires independent validation.
13	The consensus framework's structure---multi-method agreement combined with multi-seed replication---is intended to be applicable beyond this specific setting, but the operating thresholds ($K{\geq}4/5$ methods, $S{\geq}3/4$ seeds) are empirically calibrated and may require re-tuning under different conditions.
14	For instance, architectures with different tokenizer granularity or training dynamics could shift the balance between false positive and false negative rates at these thresholds.
15	Initial cross-architecture validation on GPT-2-medium (single seed, $\alpha{=}1.0$) replicates the entropy-leads-diversity pattern at Tier~3 consensus: all 6 canary$\to$diversity pairs reach $K{\geq}4/5$ method agreement (permutation Granger $p_{\text{FDR}}{=}0.003$), with 0/6 reverse-direction pairs significant.
16	Full-panel inter-method agreement remains low (Fleiss' $\kappa{=}0.091$) but rises to $\kappa{=}0.667$ among the three informative methods (\S\ref{sec:method_sensitivity}), confirming that the consensus framework's structure transfers across architectures even if operating thresholds require architecture-specific calibration.
17	At $\alpha{=}0.25$, GPT-2-medium yields 1/8 pairs at Tier~3 consensus ($K{\geq}4/5$) and 7/8 at Tier~2 ($K{\geq}3/5$), placing it in the same null/low-detectability regime as Pythia (0/8 Tier~3) and confirming that the detectability boundary is consistent across two transformer decoder-only model families of similar scale.
18	The full GPT-2-medium drift profile at $\alpha{=}0.25$ is: entropy $-32.8\%$, ECE $+422\%$, perplexity $-44.5\%$, distinct-1 $-23.5\%$, distinct-2 $-31.6\%$, distinct-3 $-30.2\%$, MAUVE $0.0315 \to 0.0282$ ($-10.5\%$).
19	Compared to Pythia ($-4.7{\pm}0.5\%$ entropy, $-40.3{\pm}0.3\%$ distinct-1), GPT-2-medium shows substantially stronger canary drift but milder diversity decline---likely due to its $8\times$ larger batch size exposing more data per step.
20	The stronger canary signal approaches detectability (permutation Granger $F{=}64.09$, $p{=}0.081$ for entropy$\to$distinct-1, compared to $p{>}0.5$ in Pythia), suggesting that the regime boundary may shift with increased effective sample size, but single-seed $T{=}10$ power remains insufficient for Confirmatory claims.
21	Toda--Yamamoto Granger finds 1/8 pairs significant, consistent with its known instability at short series lengths (\S\ref{sec:consensus_validation}).
22	
23	\paragraph{FPR/FNR calibration symmetry.}
24	Both FPR and FNR are validated on the joint method${\times}$seed dimension: FPR uses three seeds at the null-control condition ($\alpha{=}0.0$, 0/24 pairs at $K{\geq}4/5$) and FNR aggregates across four seeds at full contamination ($\alpha{=}1.0$, 6/6 reference pairs recovered).
25	Cross-seed FPR variance is negligible---all three null-control seeds independently yield 0/8 robust pairs---confirming that the consensus framework's false positive control is seed-independent.
26	Individual-method variance (Toda--Yamamoto 0--12.5\%, threshold 12.5--62.5\%) further motivates the multi-method consensus requirement: methods that appear reliable on one seed may produce false positives on another.
27	
28	\paragraph{Short time-series constraint.}
29	With $T{=}10$ generation cycles (11 data points per series), our time-series analyses operate near the lower bound of what standard methods require.
30	This series length is typical for iterative training studies, where each generation involves a complete training run, but it severely limits statistical power.
31	The Toda--Yamamoto procedure's elevated FNR (33.3\%, missing 2 of 6 directional pairs) is attributable to insufficient residual degrees of freedom at this series length, not to a fundamental deficiency in the method itself.
32	This finding aligns with \citet{boettiger2012limits}, who demonstrated that early-warning signals have limited detection power in short time series.
33	Longer generation chains would improve power for all methods but impose substantial computational cost; this trade-off between statistical power and compute budget is inherent to iterative training research and cannot be resolved by methodological improvements alone.
34	However, observation window sensitivity analysis provides reassurance: the $4/5$ consensus classification stabilizes at $T{\geq}5$, well below our observation window of $T{=}10$, with identical results from $T{=}5$ through $T{=}10$ (Appendix~\ref{app:window_sensitivity}).
35	The Toda--Yamamoto procedure produces valid test statistics at all window lengths ($T \in \{5, \ldots, 10\}$), but with unstable sensitivity---3/6 to 6/6 pairs reach significance depending on window length---confirming that its elevated FNR reflects limited residual degrees of freedom rather than absence of signal.
36	
37	\paragraph{Effective rank direction is model-dependent.}
38	Effective rank evolves in opposite directions across architectures during iterative training.
39	In Pythia-410M, effective rank decreases (representational contraction), consistent with mode collapse concentrating the output distribution into fewer dimensions.
40	In GPT-2-medium at $\alpha{=}0.25$, effective rank \emph{increases} from 2.37 to 8.48 (+258\%).
41	The likely explanation is starting-point dependence: GPT-2-medium begins near rank-2 (a highly concentrated initial representation), and iterative training disperses activation mass across more dimensions rather than concentrating it further.
42	This finding demonstrates that effective rank cannot serve as a direction-agnostic canary and that its trajectory must be interpreted relative to architecture-specific baselines.
43	We note this as a caveat on the generality of representational metrics as collapse indicators, even when the regime map (Table~\ref{tab:cross_arch_regime}) and lead-lag detectability are consistent across architectures.
44	
45	\paragraph{Diversity--calibration dissociation.}
46	Diversity metrics and calibration metrics respond to synthetic contamination in qualitatively opposite directions across the $\alpha$ spectrum.
47	Diversity collapse (distinct-$n$ decline) is most severe at low contamination ($\alpha{=}0.25$: distinct-3 declines by $58.9{\pm}0.9\%$) and attenuates at higher levels ($\alpha{=}1.0$: $-30.5\%$), while calibration degradation (ECE) increases monotonically with $\alpha$ ($+51.7\%$ at $\alpha{=}0.0$ to $+3780\%$ at $\alpha{=}1.0$).
48	This dissociation underscores the need for multi-method consensus: the regime in which a metric degrades most severely ($\alpha{=}0.25$ for diversity) does not coincide with the regime in which its temporal lead is most detectable ($\alpha{\geq}0.75$), and a single metric family cannot capture the full picture of iterative training degradation.
49	
50	\paragraph{Practical utility paradox.}
51	The canary framework exhibits a paradox: it works best where it is least needed.
52	At $\alpha{\geq}0.75$, token entropy reliably Granger-precedes diversity collapse---but at these contamination levels, degradation is already severe and likely detectable through simpler means.
53	At $\alpha{\leq}0.25$, where early warning would be most valuable, the canary signal is undetectable by any method tested; at $\alpha{=}0.50$, detectability is seed-dependent (0/8--6/8, 3~seeds, median 3/8), marking the onset boundary where ECE outperforms token entropy as the more sensitive canary.
54	This is not a framework deficiency but an inherent characteristic of collapse dynamics: at low contamination, diversity changes are too small relative to inter-generation noise for any temporal lead to emerge above the statistical detection floor~\cite{boettiger2012limits}.
55	The practical implication is that the framework is better suited to continuous monitoring dashboards---tracking cumulative trend shifts across many generations---than to single-point threshold alarms.
56	If contamination grows over time (as in realistic web-scale data pipelines), the canary signal will strengthen as $\alpha$ increases, providing earlier detection than post-hoc quality audits.
57	
58	\paragraph{Token entropy versus perplexity.}
59	The finding that token entropy Granger-precedes diversity while perplexity does not ($F{\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$ after differencing) requires clarification, since perplexity is the exponential of cross-entropy and appears mathematically related to token entropy.
60	The distinction lies in the evaluation domain: our ``token entropy'' is $H(p_\theta | \text{context})$ evaluated on \emph{held-out real data}---measuring the model's predictive uncertainty on genuine text---while ``perplexity'' is $\exp(H_\text{cross})$ evaluated on \emph{model-generated text}---measuring the model's self-consistency on its own outputs.
61	The identity $\text{ppl} = \exp(\text{entropy})$ holds only when the model distribution equals the data distribution, a condition that iterative collapse systematically violates.
62	In practice, both metrics trend downward during collapse (the model becomes more certain), producing high undifferenced correlation.
63	First-differencing removes this shared trend: the canary$\to$diversity channel survives because entropy \emph{leads} diversity decline by one generation (the model narrows its distribution before output diversity measurably falls), whereas the canary$\to$perplexity channel vanishes because entropy and perplexity co-move \emph{synchronously}---both driven by the same underlying distributional contraction.
64	Thus, the perplexity channel being ``spurious'' is not trivially expected from the mathematical relationship, but rather a substantive finding about collapse dynamics: the generation-evaluation divergence breaks the entropy--perplexity coupling in a temporally informative way.
65	
66	\paragraph{NLP-specific constraints on lead-lag analysis.}
67	Our application of Granger-type methods to iterative training faces constraints absent in their original econometric settings.
68	First, observation length: typical econometric applications use $T{=}100$--$1000$ time points, whereas iterative training studies are limited to $T{=}5$--$20$ generations due to computational cost.
69	This severely constrains degrees of freedom for VAR-based methods and explains the Toda--Yamamoto procedure's poor power in our setting.
70	Second, non-stationarity is by design: each generation's training data differs from the previous generation's, making the underlying process non-stationary by construction rather than as an incidental property.
71	Standard stationarity tests (ADF, KPSS) may yield misleading results when applied to processes with deterministic degradation trajectories rather than stochastic unit roots.
72	Third, the sample-size bottleneck compounds with multiple testing: with only 8--32 pairs per regime, FDR correction operates on small multiplicity, limiting its ability to separate true from false discoveries.
73	These constraints motivate our multi-method consensus approach as a complement to---rather than replacement for---traditional significance testing.
74	
75	\paragraph{Future work.}
76	Several directions extend this work.
77	Multivariate VAR models would enable testing for confounders and multi-directional Granger relationships beyond our current bivariate framework.
78	Cross-architecture validation beyond our single-seed GPT-2-medium validation requires multi-seed replication; evaluation on larger-scale models would further test generalizability.
79	Online monitoring---integrating canary metrics into training pipelines for real-time collapse detection---is a natural applied extension.
80	Accumulate-mode experiments~\cite{dey2024breaking} would test whether canary dynamics differ from the replace-mode setting studied here.
81	Finally, connecting the empirically observed onset boundary at $\alpha{\approx}0.25$--$0.50$ to theoretical phase transition analysis~\cite{truong2025entropy} could yield a principled explanation for why canary signals emerge at this contamination regime rather than at lower contamination levels.
```

symmetry.}\nBoth FPR and FNR are validated on the joint method${\\times}$seed dimension: FPR uses three seeds at the null-control condition ($\\alpha{=}0.0$, 0/24 pairs at $K{\\geq}4/5$) and FNR aggregates across four seeds at full contamination ($\\alpha{=}1.0$, 6/6 reference pairs recovered).\nCross-seed FPR variance is negligible---all three null-control seeds independently yield 0/8 robust pairs---confirming that the consensus framework's false positive control is seed-independent.\nIndividual-method variance (Toda--Yamamoto 0--12.5\\%, threshold 12.5--62.5\\%) further motivates the multi-method consensus requirement: methods that appear reliable on one seed may produce false positives on another.\n\n\\paragraph{Short time-series constraint.}\nWith $T{=}10$ generation cycles (11 data points per series), our time-series analyses operate near the lower bound of what standard methods require.\nThis series length is typical for iterative training studies, where each generation involves a complete training run, but it severely limits statistical power.\nThe Toda--Yamamoto procedure's elevated FNR (33.3\\%, missing 2 of 6 directional pairs) is attributable to insufficient residual degrees of freedom at this series length, not to a fundamental deficiency in the method itself.\nThis finding aligns with \\citet{boettiger2012limits}, who demonstrated that early-warning signals have limited detection power in short time series.\nLonger generation chains would improve power for all methods but impose substantial computational cost; this trade-off between statistical power and compute budget is inherent to iterative training research and cannot be resolved by methodological improvements alone.\nHowever, observation window sensitivity analysis provides reassurance: the $4/5$ consensus classification stabilizes at $T{\\geq}5$, well below our observation window of $T{=}10$, with identical results from $T{=}5$ through $T{=}10$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:window_sensitivity}).\nThe Toda--Yamamoto procedure produces valid test statistics at all window lengths ($T \\in \\{5, \\ldots, 10\\}$), but with unstable sensitivity---3/6 to 6/6 pairs reach significance depending on window length---confirming that its elevated FNR reflects limited residual degrees of freedom rather than absence of signal.\n\n\\paragraph{Effective rank direction is model-dependent.}\nEffective rank evolves in opposite directions across architectures during iterative training.\nIn Pythia-410M, effective rank decreases (representational contraction), consistent with mode collapse concentrating the output distribution into fewer dimensions.\nIn GPT-2-medium at $\\alpha{=}0.25$, effective rank \\emph{increases} from 2.37 to 8.48 (+258\\%).\nThe likely explanation is starting-point dependence: GPT-2-medium begins near rank-2 (a highly concentrated initial representation), and iterative training disperses activation mass across more dimensions rather than concentrating it further.\nThis finding demonstrates that effective rank cannot serve as a direction-agnostic canary and that its trajectory must be interpreted relative to architecture-specific baselines.\nWe note this as a caveat on the generality of representational metrics as collapse indicators, even when the regime map (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_arch_regime}) and lead-lag detectability are consistent across architectures.\n\n\\paragraph{Diversity--calibration dissociation.}\nDiversity metrics and calibration metrics respond to synthetic contamination in qualitatively opposite directions across the $\\alpha$ spectrum.\nDiversity collapse (distinct-$n$ decline) is most severe at low contamination ($\\alpha{=}0.25$: distinct-3 declines by $58.9{\\pm}0.9\\%$) and attenuates at higher levels ($\\alpha{=}1.0$: $-30.5\\%$), while calibration degradation (ECE) increases monotonically with $\\alpha$ ($+51.7\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.0$ to $+3780\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}1.0$).\nThis dissociation underscores the need for multi-method consensus: the regime in which a metric degrades most severely ($\\alpha{=}0.25$ for diversity) does not coincide with the regime in which its temporal lead is most detectable ($\\alpha{\\geq}0.75$), and a single metric family cannot capture the full picture of iterative training degradation.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical utility paradox.}\nThe canary framework exhibits a paradox: it works best where it is least needed.\nAt $\\alpha{\\geq}0.75$, token entropy reliably Granger-precedes diversity collapse---but at these contamination levels, degradation is already severe and likely detectable through simpler means.\nAt $\\alpha{\\leq}0.25$, where early warning would be most valuable, the canary signal is undetectable by any method tested; at $\\alpha{=}0.50$, detectability is seed-dependent (0/8--6/8, 3~seeds, median 3/8), marking the onset boundary where ECE outperforms token entropy as the more sensitive canary.\nThis is not a framework deficiency but an inherent characteristic of collapse dynamics: at low contamination, diversity changes are too small relative to inter-generation noise for any temporal lead to emerge above the statistical detection floor~\\cite{boettiger2012limits}.\nThe practical implication is that the framework is better suited to continuous monitoring dashboards---tracking cumulative trend shifts across many generations---than to single-point threshold alarms.\nIf contamination grows over time (as in realistic web-scale data pipelines), the canary signal will strengthen as $\\alpha$ increases, providing earlier detection than post-hoc quality audits.\n\n\\paragraph{Token entropy versus perplexity.}\nThe finding that token entropy Granger-precedes diversity while perplexity does not ($F{\\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$ after differencing) requires clarification, since perplexity is the exponential of cross-entropy and appears mathematically related to token entropy.\nThe distinction lies in the evaluation domain: our ``token entropy'' is $H(p_\\theta | \\text{context})$ evaluated on \\emph{held-out real data}---measuring the model's predictive uncertainty on genuine text---while ``perplexity'' is $\\exp(H_\\text{cross})$ evaluated on \\emph{model-generated text}---measuring the model's self-consistency on its own outputs.\nThe identity $\\text{ppl} = \\exp(\\text{entropy})$ holds only when the model distribution equals the data distribution, a condition that iterative collapse systematically violates.\nIn practice, both metrics trend downward during collapse (the model becomes more certain), producing high undifferenced correlation.\nFirst-differencing removes this shared trend: the canary$\\to$diversity channel survives because entropy \\emph{leads} diversity decline by one generation (the model narrows its distribution before output diversity measurably falls), whereas the canary$\\to$perplexity channel vanishes because entropy and perplexity co-move \\emph{synchronously}---both driven by the same underlying distributional contraction.\nThus, the perplexity channel being ``spurious'' is not trivially expected from the mathematical relationship, but rather a substantive finding about collapse dynamics: the generation-evaluation divergence breaks the entropy--perplexity coupling in a temporally informative way.\n\n\\paragraph{NLP-specific constraints on lead-lag analysis.}\nOur application of Granger-type methods to iterative training faces constraints absent in their original econometric settings.\nFirst, observation length: typical econometric applications use $T{=}100$--$1000$ time points, whereas iterative training studies are limited to $T{=}5$--$20$ generations due to computational cost.\nThis severely constrains degrees of freedom for VAR-based methods and explains the Toda--Yamamoto procedure's poor power in our setting.\nSecond, non-stationarity is by design: each generation's training data differs from the previous generation's, making the underlying process non-stationary by construction rather than as an incidental property.\nStandard stationarity tests (ADF, KPSS) may yield misleading results when applied to processes with deterministic degradation trajectories rather than stochastic unit roots.\nThird, the sample-size bottleneck compounds with multiple testing: with only 8--32 pairs per regime, FDR correction operates on small multiplicity, limiting its ability to separate true from false discoveries.\nThese constraints motivate our multi-method consensus approach as a complement to---rather than replacement for---traditional significance testing.\n\n\\paragraph{Future work.}\nSeveral directions extend this work.\nMultivariate VAR models would enable testing for confounders and multi-directional Granger relationships beyond our current bivariate framework.\nCross-architecture validation beyond our single-seed GPT-2-medium validation requires multi-seed replication; evaluation on larger-scale models would further test generalizability.\nOnline monitoring---integrating canary metrics into training pipelines for real-time collapse detection---is a natural applied extension.\nAccumulate-mode experiments~\\cite{dey2024breaking} would test whether canary dynamics differ from the replace-mode setting studied here.\nFinally, connecting the empirically observed onset boundary at $\\alpha{\\approx}0.25$--$0.50$ to theoretical phase transition analysis~\\cite{truong2025entropy} could yield a principled explanation for why canary signals emerge at this contamination regime rather than at lower contamination levels.",

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	Lead-lag analysis in iterative training collapse is far more fragile than the current literature assumes.
5	Method choice alone inflates spurious-signal rates to at least 62.5\%, first-differencing exposes canary$\to$perplexity associations as entirely spurious, and regime dependence causes the same Granger test to shift from 24/24 to 0/8 significant pairs across contamination levels.
6	These pitfalls establish that single-method, single-regime analyses---the current default---cannot reliably distinguish genuine temporal precedence from statistical artifacts.
7	
8	Our multi-method consensus framework addresses this fragility by requiring convergent evidence across multiple independent methods and random seeds.
9	Calibrated on effect-size reference pairs with a non-circular 4-method panel, the framework achieves FPR${=}$0\% (0/24, three null-control seeds) and FNR${=}$0\% with permutation Granger power${=}1.0$, and four tiers of practical guidance scaling from exploratory screening to publication-grade claims.
10	
11	Applying the framework, token entropy exhibits robust Granger-precedence over diversity collapse at $\alpha{=}1.0$ (24/24 forward, 0/24 reverse, Tier~3 across 4~seeds and GPT-2-medium replication) with a consistent one-generation lead; at $\alpha{=}0.75$, directional asymmetry is confirmed (19/24 forward, 0/24 reverse across 3~seeds) but method consensus remains transitional (Tier~2--3), mapping the detectability boundary.
12	This finding is deliberately scoped to lexical diversity, not model collapse broadly, since the perplexity channel proved entirely spurious under differencing.
13	
14	The methodological pitfalls we expose arise from generic properties of short, non-stationary time series, and we conjecture the consensus framework transfers to other iterative training settings where multiple detection methods can be applied in parallel.
15	Limitations are detailed in \S\ref{sec:limitations}; future directions are discussed in \S\ref{sec:discussion}.
```

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	Several limitations constrain the generalizability of our findings.
5	
6	\paragraph{Model scale.}
7	Our primary experiments use Pythia-410M (410M parameters); cross-architecture validation (single-seed GPT-2-medium, 345M parameters).
8	Both are decoder-only transformers of similar scale, small by contemporary standards.
9	Whether the entropy-leads-diversity pattern and the consensus framework's operating thresholds hold at billion-parameter scale remains untested.
10	The batch size difference between architectures (4 for Pythia vs.\ 32 for GPT-2-medium) means each training step exposes $8\times$ more data in the latter, potentially accelerating collapse dynamics without altering the qualitative pattern.
11	
12	\paragraph{Data domain.}
13	Training data is drawn from a single domain (a WikiText-103 subset), and domain-specific distributional properties---vocabulary richness, syntactic complexity, topical diversity---may influence the coupling between canary and downstream metrics.
14	Domains with inherently low diversity (e.g., legal text, code) could exhibit different canary dynamics.
15	
16	\paragraph{Short time series ($T{=}10$).}
17	With 11 data points per series, our statistical analyses operate near the lower bound of what standard methods require.
18	This limits power for all methods and makes the Toda--Yamamoto procedure particularly unreliable (power estimated at 0.05--0.20 for our effect sizes).
19	We omit transfer entropy---a standard nonlinear Granger alternative---because kernel density estimation requires substantially longer time series (typically $T{\geq}100$) than our 10-generation design permits, yielding negligible statistical power at $T{=}10$.
20	Longer generation chains would improve statistical power but impose substantial computational cost.
21	
22	\paragraph{Bivariate Granger $\neq$ causality.}
23	Bivariate Granger tests cannot exclude confounders.
24	An unobserved process---such as a latent shift in the training data distribution---could independently drive both token entropy and diversity metrics, producing spurious lead-lag associations.
25	Our multi-method consensus framework mitigates but does not eliminate this limitation; multivariate VAR models would be required to test for confounders directly.
26	
27	\paragraph{Regime boundary uncertainty.}
28	The onset boundary at $\alpha{\approx}0.25$--$0.50$ is characterized with limited resolution (five $\alpha$ levels).
29	Whether this boundary is sharp (phase-transition-like) or gradual, and whether it shifts with model scale or data properties, remains open.
30	Three-seed replication at $\alpha{=}0.75$ confirms directional asymmetry (19/24 forward, 0/24 reverse) but method consensus remains transitional (Tier~2--3), leaving the exact boundary position uncertain.
31	
32	\paragraph{No intervention experiment.}
33	We observe that token entropy Granger-precedes diversity collapse but do not test whether acting on canary signals (e.g., halting training or reducing $\alpha$) can prevent or delay collapse onset.
34	Without intervention evidence, the practical value of the canary remains theoretical.
35	
36	\paragraph{Replace-only protocol.}
37	Our experiments use replace-only data accumulation; accumulate-mode training~\cite{dey2024breaking,kazdan2025collapse} may exhibit qualitatively different canary dynamics or eliminate the need for canary monitoring entirely~\cite{falahati2026curated}.
38	
39	\paragraph{Scope limited to diversity metrics.}
40	Our canary claims are scoped to distinct-$n$ diversity metrics~\cite{li2016diversity}---not to model collapse broadly~\cite{schaeffer2025position}---as the perplexity channel proved spurious after differencing.
41	This scoping is theoretically motivated by the calibration-diversity tradeoff~\cite{cao2025entropycal}, but means our findings may not generalize to other collapse manifestations (e.g., factual degradation, reasoning capability loss).
42	
```

```text
5	All scientific claims, experimental design decisions, data analysis, and interpretations are the sole responsibility of the authors.

8	\paragraph{Responsible NLP considerations.}
9	This work studies degradation in iterative language model training.
10	We do not release models trained on synthetic data, as they exhibit reduced diversity and calibration.
11	Our findings highlight a failure mode relevant to the responsible deployment of language models: unmonitored iterative training on web-crawled data (which increasingly contains LLM outputs) may degrade model quality in ways detectable only through the multi-method framework we propose.
12	We encourage practitioners to implement canary monitoring in production training pipelines as a safeguard against silent quality degradation.
```

```text
1	\appendix
2	
3	\section{Distinct-3 Results}
4	\label{app:distinct3}
5	
6	Table~\ref{tab:distinct3_drift} reports distinct-3 drift across all contamination levels.
7	Distinct-3 exhibits the largest absolute decline among diversity metrics at low contamination ($-58.9{\pm}0.9\%$ at $\alpha{=}0.25$) but attenuates at higher levels ($-30.5\%$ at $\alpha{=}1.0$), consistent with the diversity--calibration dissociation discussed in \S\ref{sec:discussion}.
8	
9	\begin{table}[h]
10	\centering
11	\small
12	\caption{Distinct-3 drift (\%, generation~0$\to$10) across contamination levels. $\alpha{=}0.00$, $\alpha{=}0.25$, $\alpha{=}0.75$: 3-seed mean${\pm}$std; $\alpha{=}1.0$: 4-seed mean${\pm}$std; $\alpha{=}0.50$: single seed.}
13	\label{tab:distinct3_drift}
14	\begin{tabular}{lc}
15	\toprule
16	$\alpha$ & Distinct-3 drift (\%) \\
17	\midrule
18	0.00 & $-4.4{\pm}0.5$ \\
19	0.25 & $-58.9{\pm}0.9$ \\
20	0.50 & $-52.4$ \\
21	0.75 & $-38.6{\pm}1.3$ \\
22	1.00 & $-30.5{\pm}4.0$ \\
23	\bottomrule
24	\end{tabular}
25	\end{table}
26	
27	\paragraph{Lead-lag analysis.}
28	At $\alpha{=}1.0$, token entropy Granger-precedes distinct-3 at the same consensus level as distinct-1 and distinct-2: $K{\geq}4/5$ methods, $S{\geq}3/4$ seeds (Tier~3).
29	However, the optimal lag is less stable than for distinct-1/2: at max\_lag${=}7$, the lag standard deviation across seeds exceeds 1.5 generations, compared to $<0.5$ for distinct-1/2.
30	This instability reflects bimodal lag distributions (modes at lag~1 and lag~3--4), suggesting that distinct-3 captures two temporally distinct diversity loss mechanisms: rapid n-gram homogenization (lag~1) and slower distributional narrowing (lag~3--4).
31	
32	\paragraph{Sensitivity to sequence length.}
33	Higher-order $n$-gram diversity metrics are more sensitive to generation length and random sampling variability.
34	With our 512-token generation length, distinct-3 has a smaller effective denominator (number of possible unique trigrams observed) than distinct-1 or distinct-2, amplifying stochastic fluctuations.
35	This partially explains the higher lag instability: the signal-to-noise ratio for temporal precedence is lower for distinct-3, requiring more observations (longer $T$ or more seeds) to achieve stable estimates.
36	For this reason, we report distinct-3 in this appendix rather than in the main text, where distinct-1 and distinct-2 provide cleaner evidence of the entropy-leads-diversity pattern.
37	
38	
39	\section{Observation Window Sensitivity}
40	\label{app:window_sensitivity}
41	
42	Table~\ref{tab:window_sensitivity} reports how the Tier~3 consensus classification ($K{\geq}4/5$ methods) varies as a function of the minimum observation window $T_{\min}$, where analysis uses only generations $0$ through $T_{\min}$.
43	The consensus classification stabilizes at $T_{\min}{\geq}5$ (6 data points), well below our full observation window of $T{=}10$: all six effect-size reference pairs (token entropy and ECE $\to$ distinct-1, distinct-2, distinct-3) reach $K{\geq}4/5$ agreement at every window length from $T_{\min}{=}5$ through $T_{\min}{=}10$, with no spurious pairs (canary$\to$perplexity) admitted.
44	
45	Toda--Yamamoto is the only method exhibiting window-dependent sensitivity: its significant-pair count ranges from 3/6 to 6/6 reference pairs depending on $T_{\min}$, reflecting insufficient residual degrees of freedom at shorter windows.
46	This instability does not affect the consensus classification because the remaining four methods provide stable $K{\geq}4/5$ agreement independently of Toda--Yamamoto's vote.
47	
48	\begin{table}[h]
49	\centering
50	\small
51	\caption{Observation window sensitivity for Tier~3 consensus ($K{\geq}4/5$) at $\alpha{=}1.0$ (Pythia-410M, 4~seeds). Tier~3 pairs: number of canary$\to$downstream pairs (out of 8 validated) reaching $K{\geq}4/5$ method agreement. TY pairs: number of effect-size reference pairs (out of 6) reaching Toda--Yamamoto significance. Consensus stabilizes at $T_{\min}{\geq}5$; TY remains unstable throughout.}
52	\label{tab:window_sensitivity}
53	\begin{tabular}{lccccccccc}
54	\toprule
55	& \multicolumn{8}{c}{Minimum observation window $T_{\min}$} \\
56	\cmidrule(lr){2-9}
57	& 3 & 4 & 5 & 6 & 7 & 8 & 9 & 10 \\
58	\midrule
59	Tier~3 pairs (/8)    & $\dagger$ & $\dagger$ & 6 & 6 & 6 & 6 & 6 & 6 \\
60	TY pairs (/6)        & $\dagger$ & $\dagger$ & 3--6 & 3--6 & 3--6 & 3--6 & 3--6 & 6 \\
61	Stable               & ---       & ---       & \checkmark & \checkmark & \checkmark & \checkmark & \checkmark & \checkmark \\
62	\bottomrule
63	\multicolumn{9}{l}{\footnotesize $\dagger$~Insufficient degrees of freedom for Granger-type tests ($<6$ data points).}
64	\end{tabular}
65	\end{table}
66	
67	
68	\section{Robustness Checks}
69	\label{app:robustness}
70	
71	\paragraph{Permutation count stability.}
72	Table~\ref{tab:nperm_stability} compares permutation Granger $p$-values at 5{,}000 versus 10{,}000 permutations across all canary$\to$downstream pairs at $\alpha{=}0.75$ (3~seeds, 24~pairs).
73	Permutation Granger $p$-values are stable to within 0.002 between 5{,}000 and 10{,}000 permutations, with zero significance decisions affected.
74	
75	\begin{table}[h]
76	\centering
77	\small
78	\caption{Permutation count stability analysis ($\alpha{=}0.75$, 3~seeds, 24 canary$\to$downstream pairs). $p$-values from permutation Granger tests at $n_{\text{perm}}{=}5{,}000$ versus $n_{\text{perm}}{=}10{,}000$.}
79	\label{tab:nperm_stability}
80	\begin{tabular}{lc}
81	\toprule
82	Metric & Value \\
83	\midrule
84	Max $|\Delta p|$ & 0.010 \\
85	Mean $|\Delta p|$ & 0.002 \\
86	Significance flips & 0 / 24 \\
87	\bottomrule
88	\end{tabular}
89	\end{table}
90	
91	\paragraph{FDR threshold sensitivity.}
92	Table~\ref{tab:fdr_sensitivity} reports the effect of varying the BH FDR threshold $q$ on treatment ($\alpha{=}0.75$, 3~seeds) versus null ($\alpha{=}0.0$, 3~seeds) separation for permutation Granger tests.
93	Treatment/null separation is qualitatively robust to FDR threshold choice; the null control remains at 0/24 across all thresholds.
94	
95	\begin{table}[h]
96	\centering
97	\small
98	\caption{FDR threshold sensitivity for permutation Granger significance. Treatment: $\alpha{=}0.75$ (3~seeds, 24 forward-direction pairs). Null: $\alpha{=}0.0$ (3~seeds, 24 pairs). The null control produces zero false positives at all thresholds tested.}
99	\label{tab:fdr_sensitivity}
100	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
101	\toprule
102	$q$ & Null FDR-sig (/24) & Treatment FDR-sig (/24) & Separation \\
103	\midrule
104	0.01 & 0 & 0  & Clean (underpowered) \\
105	0.05 & 0 & 19 & Clean \\
106	0.10 & 0 & 24 & Clean \\
107	\bottomrule
108	\end{tabular}
109	\end{table}
```

```json
  "account": "root"
```

```json
{
```
```json
  "server": "westd-19828",
  "command": "cat << 'PAPER_EOF' > /root/autodl-tmp/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/conclusion.tex\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nLead-lag analysis in iterative training collapse is far more fragile than the current literature assumes.\nMethod choice alone inflates spurious-signal rates to at least 62.5\\%, first-differencing exposes canary$\\to$perplexity associations as entirely spurious, and regime dependence causes the same Granger test to shift from 24/24 to 0/8 significant pairs across contamination levels.\nThese pitfalls establish that single-method, single-regime analyses---the current default---cannot reliably distinguish genuine temporal precedence from statistical artifacts.\n\nOur multi-method consensus framework addresses this fragility by requiring convergent evidence across multiple independent methods and random seeds.\nCalibrated on effect-size reference pairs with a non-circular 4-method panel, the framework achieves FPR${=}$0\\% (0/24, three null-control seeds) and FNR${=}$0\\% with permutation Granger power${=}1.0$, and four tiers of practical guidance scaling from exploratory screening to publication-grade claims.\n\nApplying the framework, token entropy exhibits robust Granger-precedence over diversity collapse at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (24/24 forward, 0/24 reverse, Tier~3 across 4~seeds and GPT-2-medium replication) with a consistent one-generation lead; at $\\alpha{=}0.75$, directional asymmetry is confirmed (19/24 forward, 0/24 reverse across 3~seeds) but method consensus remains transitional (Tier~2--3), mapping the detectability boundary.\nThis finding is deliberately scoped to lexical diversity, not model collapse broadly, since the perplexity channel proved entirely spurious under differencing.\n\nThe methodological pitfalls we expose arise from generic properties of short, non-stationary time series, and we conjecture the consensus framework transfers to other iterative training settings where multiple detection methods can be applied in parallel.\nLimitations are detailed in \\S\\ref{sec:limitations}; future directions are discussed in \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}.\nPAPER_EOF",
  "account": "root"
}
```

```json
  "command": "cat << 'PAPER_EOF' > /root/autodl-tmp/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/limitations.tex\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nSeveral limitations constrain the generalizability of our findings.\n\n\\paragraph{Model scale.}\nOur primary experiments use Pythia-410M (410M parameters); cross-architecture validation (single-seed GPT-2-medium, 345M parameters).\nBoth are decoder-only transformers of similar scale, small by contemporary standards.\nWhether the entropy-leads-diversity pattern and the consensus framework's operating thresholds hold at billion-parameter scale remains untested.\nThe batch size difference between architectures (4 for Pythia vs.\\ 32 for GPT-2-medium) means each training step exposes $8\\times$ more data in the latter, potentially accelerating collapse dynamics without altering the qualitative pattern.\n\n\\paragraph{Data domain.}\nTraining data is drawn from a single domain (a WikiText-103 subset), and domain-specific distributional properties---vocabulary richness, syntactic complexity, topical diversity---may influence the coupling between canary and downstream metrics.\nDomains with inherently low diversity (e.g., legal text, code) could exhibit different canary dynamics.\n\n\\paragraph{Short time series ($T{=}10$).}\nWith 11 data points per series, our statistical analyses operate near the lower bound of what standard methods require.\nThis limits power for all methods and makes the Toda--Yamamoto procedure particularly unreliable (power estimated at 0.05--0.20 for our effect sizes).\nWe omit transfer entropy---a standard nonlinear Granger alternative---because kernel density estimation requires substantially longer time series (typically $T{\\geq}100$) than our 10-generation design permits, yielding negligible statistical power at $T{=}10$.\nLonger generation chains would improve statistical power but impose substantial computational cost.\n\n\\paragraph{Bivariate Granger $\\neq$ causality.}\nBivariate Granger tests cannot exclude confounders.\nAn unobserved process---such as a latent shift in the training data distribution---could independently drive both token entropy and diversity metrics, producing spurious lead-lag associations.\nOur multi-method consensus framework mitigates but does not eliminate this limitation; multivariate VAR models would be required to test for confounders directly.\n\n\\paragraph{Regime boundary uncertainty.}\nThe onset boundary at $\\alpha{\\approx}0.25$--$0.50$ is characterized with limited resolution (five $\\alpha$ levels).\nWhether this boundary is sharp (phase-transition-like) or gradual, and whether it shifts with model scale or data properties, remains open.\nThree-seed replication at $\\alpha{=}0.75$ confirms directional asymmetry (19/24 forward, 0/24 reverse) but method consensus remains transitional (Tier~2--3), leaving the exact boundary position uncertain.\n\n\\paragraph{No intervention experiment.}\nWe observe that token entropy Granger-precedes diversity collapse but do not test whether acting on canary signals (e.g., halting training or reducing $\\alpha$) can prevent or delay collapse onset.\nWithout intervention evidence, the practical value of the canary remains theoretical.\n\n\\paragraph{Replace-only protocol.}\nOur experiments use replace-only data accumulation; accumulate-mode training~\\cite{dey2024breaking,kazdan2025collapse} may exhibit qualitatively different canary dynamics or eliminate the need for canary monitoring entirely~\\cite{falahati2026curated}.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope limited to diversity metrics.}\nOur canary claims are scoped to distinct-$n$ diversity metrics~\\cite{li2016diversity}---not to model collapse broadly~\\cite{schaeffer2025position}---as the perplexity channel proved spurious after differencing.\nThis scoping is theoretically motivated by the calibration-diversity tradeoff~\\cite{cao2025entropycal}, but means our findings may not generalize to other collapse manifestations (e.g., factual degradation, reasoning capability loss).\nPAPER_EOF",
```

```json
{
```
```json
  "server": "westd-19828",
  "command": "cat << 'PAPER_EOF' > /root/autodl-tmp/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/background.tex\n\\section{Background}\n\\label{sec:background}\n\nThis section establishes the formal setup and notation used throughout the paper.\n\n\\paragraph{Iterative training loop.}\nLet $M_0$ denote a pretrained language model and $\\mathcal{D}_\\text{real}$ a fixed corpus of human-written text.\nAt each generation $t \\geq 1$, the model $M_{t-1}$ generates a synthetic corpus $\\mathcal{D}_\\text{synth}^{(t-1)}$, and the next-generation model $M_t$ is fine-tuned on a mixture of $\\alpha$ fraction synthetic data and $(1{-}\\alpha)$ fraction real data:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\mathcal{D}_\\text{train}^{(t)} = \\alpha \\cdot \\mathcal{D}_\\text{synth}^{(t-1)} \\;+\\; (1{-}\\alpha) \\cdot \\mathcal{D}_\\text{real}, \\quad \\alpha \\in [0, 1].\n    \\label{eq:mixing}\n\\end{equation}\nThe contamination level $\\alpha$ controls the fraction of machine-generated text in each training round.\nAt $\\alpha{=}1.0$, this reduces to the full-replacement paradigm of \\citet{shumailov2024curse}, where each generation trains exclusively on the previous generation's output.\nIterating this process for $T$ generations yields a trajectory $M_0 \\to M_1 \\to \\cdots \\to M_T$.\n\n\\paragraph{Metric families.}\nWe distinguish two families of metrics evaluated at each generation~$t$.\n\\emph{Canary metrics} are cheap-to-compute statistics on the model's output distribution, hypothesized to change before downstream quality degrades---analogous to early-warning indicators studied in the context of critical transitions in dynamical systems \\cite{scheffer2009early}.\nWe consider two canary metrics: \\emph{token entropy}, the average next-token prediction entropy over held-out samples, and \\emph{expected calibration error} (ECE), which measures the discrepancy between predicted token probabilities and empirical correctness frequencies.\n\\emph{Downstream metrics} capture generation quality directly.\nWe use \\emph{distinct-$n$} ($n \\in \\{1, 2, 3\\}$), the ratio of unique $n$-grams to total $n$-grams in generated text \\cite{li2016diversity}, as a measure of lexical diversity, and \\emph{perplexity} on a held-out set as a measure of language modeling quality.\nEach metric produces a time series of $T{+}1$ observations (generations $0$ through $T$).\n\n\\paragraph{The lead-lag testing problem.}\nGiven two such time series $X = (x_0, \\ldots, x_T)$ and $Y = (y_0, \\ldots, y_T)$---for instance, a canary metric and a downstream metric---the central question is whether changes in $X$ temporally precede changes in $Y$.\nThis requires more than measuring correlation: the test must establish \\emph{directionality} ($X \\to Y$ rather than $Y \\to X$) and account for confounders such as shared non-stationary trends that can produce spurious associations.\nThe classical framework for testing temporal predictive precedence is Granger causality \\cite{granger1969investigating}, which asks whether past values of $X$ improve prediction of $Y$ beyond $Y$'s own history.\nWe note that Granger precedence tests predictive improvement, not true causality \\cite{shojaie2022granger}; this distinction is discussed further in \\S\\ref{sec:method}.\n\n\\paragraph{Five detection methods.}\nWe employ five lead-lag detection methods spanning a spectrum from permissive to conservative:\n(1)~\\emph{naive cross-correlation}, which computes Pearson correlation on undifferenced series at lags up to~5;\n(2)~\\emph{differenced cross-correlation}, which applies the same procedure to first-differenced series, removing shared linear trends;\n(3)~\\emph{threshold onset detection}, which compares the generation at which each metric first exceeds a predefined deviation threshold;\n(4)~\\emph{permutation Granger causality}, a non-parametric Granger test using 5{,}000 permutations on first-differenced series \\cite{shojaie2022granger}; and\n(5)~\\emph{Toda--Yamamoto Granger causality}, an augmented VAR approach that tests Granger precedence without requiring prior differencing \\cite{toda1995statistical}.\nThese methods differ in their assumptions about stationarity, their sensitivity to shared trends, and their statistical power at short time-series lengths.\nTheir joint application forms the basis of the multi-method consensus framework developed in \\S\\ref{sec:method}.\nPAPER_EOF",
  "account": "root"
}
```
