{
  "server": "westd-19828",
  "command": "cat << 'PAPER_EOF' > /root/autodl-tmp/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/introduction.tex\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nPractitioners monitoring iterative training pipelines face a diagnostic blind spot: the metrics most commonly used to detect distributional degradation can yield false positive rates of 62.5--100\\% when applied without proper statistical controls, yet no established protocol exists for distinguishing genuine early-warning signals from artifacts of shared non-stationary trends.\nThis blind spot is consequential because iterative training collapse, where models are unwittingly trained on output from earlier generations, is increasingly likely as machine-generated text proliferates on the web~\\cite{shumailov2024curse,alemohammad2024mad}.\nTail modes vanish, lexical diversity narrows, and calibration deteriorates across generations.\nThese failure modes motivate the search for cheap-to-compute \\emph{canary metrics} that provide early warning, analogous to early-warning signals for critical transitions in ecological and climate systems~\\cite{scheffer2009early,scheffer2012anticipating}.\n\nSeveral recent studies have pursued this direction: \\citet{du2026geometric} informally observe that expected calibration error rises before accuracy degrades; SIGMA~\\cite{sigma2026spectral} proposes spectral indicators of distributional shift; and DCES~\\cite{dces2026counter} uses center-edge sampling dynamics as a collapse signal.\nYet the statistical methodology underlying these lead-lag claims---whether a canary metric genuinely \\emph{precedes} downstream degradation, or merely correlates with it under shared non-stationary trends---has received little scrutiny.\n\nWe identify several methodological deficiencies that pervade existing lead-lag analyses of iterative training collapse:\n(1)~no differencing tests to rule out spurious regression arising from shared non-stationary trends---a well-known hazard when correlating co-trending time series;\n(2)~no multi-method cross-validation---conclusions rest on a single statistical method whose implicit assumptions about stationarity and directionality are rarely examined;\n(3)~no multiple comparison correction (e.g., Benjamini--Hochberg FDR control), increasing the risk of false discoveries when testing many metric pairs simultaneously; and\n(4)~no regime-dependent analysis, despite the possibility that the same statistical test yields opposite conclusions at different contamination levels.\nThe closest predecessor is \\citet{du2026geometric}, who observe that calibration error rises before accuracy degrades in iteratively trained language models.\nTheir analysis, however, is limited to a single metric pair, spans only 1--3 generations, and reports informal visual comparisons without statistical tests---leaving open the question of whether the observed lead is genuine or an artifact of shared trends.\nThe early-warning framework of \\citet{scheffer2009early,scheffer2012anticipating}---widely adopted in ecology for anticipating tipping points---provides the theoretical motivation for canary-based prediction, but has not been systematically applied to iterative training collapse with the methodological rigor that short, noisy time series demand~\\cite{boettiger2012limits}.\n\nWe address these gaps through a systematic case study of canary prediction of diversity collapse---the progressive loss of lexical diversity in model-generated text across training generations.\nUsing Pythia-410M across five contamination levels ($\\alpha \\in \\{0.00, 0.25, 0.50, 0.75, 1.00\\}$) and up to four random seeds, we expose three methodological pitfalls that undermine naive lead-lag analysis:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Method sensitivity.} Applied to the same data (16 candidate canary$\\to$downstream pairs, including candidates later excluded; \\S\\ref{sec:case_study}), five lead-lag detection methods yield FDR significance rates ranging from 100\\% (naive cross-correlation) to 25\\% (Toda--Yamamoto Granger causality), with inter-method agreement of Cohen's $\\kappa{=}0.185$ (poor agreement).\n    \\item \\textbf{Spurious regression.} Canary$\\to$perplexity associations appear near-universal in undifferenced analysis but vanish entirely after first-differencing ($F{\\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$), revealing them as artifacts of co-trending rather than genuine temporal precedence.\n    \\item \\textbf{Regime dependence.} Permutation Granger tests find 24/24 canary$\\to$diversity pairs FDR-significant at full synthetic replacement ($\\alpha{=}1.0$) but 0/24 at $\\alpha{\\leq}0.25$ (three seeds), with seed-dependent onset at $\\alpha{=}0.50$ (0/8--6/8, three seeds), demonstrating that detectability depends sharply on both contamination level and training stochasticity.\n\\end{enumerate}\nTo move beyond single-method analysis, we propose a multi-method consensus framework with empirically calibrated thresholds, requiring agreement across multiple independent statistical methods and random seeds before classifying a lead-lag relationship as robust.\n\nThe framework's utility is demonstrated by its calibration on built-in experimental controls.\nNaive cross-correlation identifies 100\\% of canary--collapse metric pairs as FDR-significant; the consensus pipeline (requiring ${\\geq}4/5$ methods and ${\\geq}3/4$ seeds) retains only those pairs meeting effect-size criteria calibrated against the null control.\nOn reference extremes, the framework achieves a false positive rate of 0\\% at the $\\alpha{=}0.0$ null control (0/24 pairs, three seeds) (where individual methods reach up to 100\\% FPR) and a false negative rate of 0\\% against six effect-size reference pairs at $\\alpha{=}1.0$.\nApplying the framework, we identify token entropy as a one-generation-ahead canary for diversity collapse at $\\alpha{=}1.0$: the full consensus pipeline confirms directional asymmetry with cross-correlation $|r|{>}0.99$, 24/24 forward-direction pairs FDR-significant, and 0/24 in the reverse direction.\nThree-seed replication at $\\alpha{=}0.75$ extends this finding (19/24 forward, 0/24 reverse via permutation Granger), though method-level consensus remains transitional (Tier~2--3).\nThe onset boundary of causal detectability lies at $\\alpha{\\approx}0.25$--$0.50$, below which all seeds agree on null.\nWe note that Granger precedence establishes temporal predictive improvement, not true causality~\\cite{shojaie2022granger}.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Systematic exposure of three methodological pitfalls} (method sensitivity, spurious regression, regime dependence) in lead-lag analysis of iterative training collapse.\n    Method choice alone inflates spurious-signal rates to at least 62.5\\%; first-differencing eliminates canary$\\to$perplexity associations entirely; and the same Granger test shifts from 24/24 FDR-significant pairs to 0/8 across contamination levels.\n    These results demonstrate that single-method, single-regime analyses---the current norm in this literature---are unreliable.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{A multi-method consensus framework} that functions as a diagnostic filter rather than a majority vote: the $K{\\geq}4/5$ threshold is the unique operating point where both FPR and FNR reach 0\\% on reference extremes ($K{=}3$: FPR${=}$37.5\\%; $K{=}5$: FNR${=}$50\\%).\n    Shapley-value decomposition of method contributions reveals that permutation Granger dominates the ensemble (Shapley${=}{+}0.375$) while Toda--Yamamoto contributes negatively; these diagnostics provide principled guidance for method panel selection.\n    The framework achieves FNR${=}$0\\% on effect-size reference pairs (4-method panel, permutation Granger power${=}1.0$) with four-tier practical guidance.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Identification of token entropy as a robust one-generation-ahead canary} for diversity collapse at $\\alpha{=}1.0$, confirmed by 4 independent methods, 4 seeds, and cross-architecture replication on GPT-2-medium, with complete directional asymmetry (24/24 forward, 0/24 reverse); directional asymmetry extends to $\\alpha{=}0.75$ across 3~seeds (19/24 forward, 0/24 reverse) in a transitional consensus regime (Tier~2--3). The claim is scoped to diversity metrics (distinct-$n$), not model collapse broadly.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nThe remainder of this paper is organized as follows.\n\\S\\ref{sec:related_work} surveys related work on model collapse, early-warning signals, and lead-lag methodology.\n\\S\\ref{sec:background} establishes the formal setup, notation, and the five detection methods used throughout.\n\\S\\ref{sec:method} distills the three methodological pitfalls into a unified diagnostic framework, presents the multi-method consensus pipeline, and reports its empirical calibration.\n\\S\\ref{sec:experiments} provides full experimental results: method sensitivity analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:method_sensitivity}), regime-dependent detectability (\\S\\ref{sec:regime}), a case study of canary prediction of diversity collapse (\\S\\ref{sec:case_study}), and consensus framework validation (\\S\\ref{sec:consensus_validation}).\n\\S\\ref{sec:discussion} discusses implications---including the practical utility paradox, the token entropy--perplexity distinction, and NLP-specific constraints on lead-lag analysis---and outlines directions for future work.\n\\S\\ref{sec:conclusion} concludes, followed by a dedicated Limitations section (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}).\nPAPER_EOF",

{
  "server": "westd-19828",
  "command": "cat << 'PAPER_EOF' > /root/autodl-tmp/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/related_work.tex\n\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Model collapse in iterative training.}\n\nThe phenomenon of iterative training collapse was formalized by \\citet{shumailov2024curse}, who showed that recursively replacing real data with model-generated text causes progressive tail erosion across VAEs, GMMs, and language models, and by \\citet{alemohammad2024mad}, who demonstrated quality--diversity tradeoffs in self-consuming generative loops for image models.\nTheoretical analyses have since sharpened these results: \\citet{dohmatob2024tale} recast collapse as a change in scaling-law exponents, showing that even small fractions of synthetic data alter effective scaling behavior, while \\citet{dohmatob2025strong} proved that collapse can occur even when synthetic data constitutes a vanishingly small fraction of the training set.\n\\citet{hataya2023will} provided early empirical evidence that large-scale generative models degrade future datasets through web-scale contamination, showing that even state-of-the-art models produce training-data artifacts detectable in downstream models---a finding that motivates our focus on contamination-level-dependent analysis.\n\\citet{seddik2024bad} formalized the statistical analysis of training on synthetic data, deriving excess risk bounds that quantify how synthetic fraction and model misspecification jointly govern degradation; their framework predicts regime-dependent behavior consistent with the onset boundary we observe empirically at $\\alpha{\\approx}0.25$--$0.50$.\nThe role of data accumulation has been a central theme.\n\\citet{dey2024breaking} proved that accumulating data across generations---rather than replacing it---prevents divergence of population risk in GMMs, HMMs, and language models.\n\\citet{kazdan2025collapse} systematically compared replace and accumulate regimes, finding that synthetic data value depends on the absolute quantity of co-present real data.\n\\citet{fu2025selfconsuming} provided generalization bounds for self-consuming loops, establishing that a constant fraction of real data suffices for convergence under accumulation.\nOn the calibration side, \\citet{he2025golden} showed that optimal real-data weighting converges to the golden-ratio inverse ($1/\\varphi \\approx 0.618$) in Gaussian and GLM settings, and that the risk landscape is non-monotone in mixing ratio---a theoretical prediction with implications for regime-dependent canary behavior.\nBeyond distributional statistics, \\citet{guo2024linguistic} documented progressive decline in lexical, syntactic, and semantic diversity across iterative fine-tuning generations, providing the multi-level diversity metrics that serve as downstream targets in our lead-lag analysis.\n\\citet{schaeffer2025position} catalogued eight distinct and sometimes contradictory definitions of ``model collapse'' in the literature, underscoring the importance of explicit operational definitions---a concern our framework addresses by scoping canary prediction to diversity metrics specifically.\n\n\\paragraph{Canary metrics and collapse monitoring.}\n\nSeveral recent works have proposed monitoring metrics that precede or accompany distributional degradation during iterative training.\n\\citet{du2026geometric} observe that calibration error rises before accuracy degrades in iteratively trained language models, interpreting this through a geometric-regulation lens.\n\\citet{du2025scales} report a similar pattern across model scales and mixing ratios, noting that ECE increases of 75\\% appear at 30\\% synthetic contamination before accuracy drops become visible.\nBeyond calibration, SIGMA~\\cite{sigma2026spectral} introduces a dual-track spectral framework based on embedding Gram matrices, where divergence between deterministic and stochastic spectral bounds serves as an early collapse diagnostic.\n\\citet{kalinowski2026topology} proposes a topological monitoring approach using persistent homology features to detect multi-scale structural loss in representation space.\nDCES~\\cite{dces2026counter} uses center-edge sampling dynamics as a collapse signal and reports improvements in ECE and entropy preservation.\n\\citet{gambetta2024surplexity} introduces surplexity---a surprise-based metric that simultaneously detects collapse and filters training data---demonstrating that high-surplexity selection matches human-data baselines in maintaining distributional balance.\n\nThese works collectively demonstrate growing interest in collapse monitoring, yet they share methodological limitations that weaken their lead-lag claims.\nNone apply differencing tests to distinguish genuine temporal precedence from spurious co-trending; none cross-validate findings across multiple statistical methods; and none apply false discovery rate correction when testing multiple metric pairs.\nOur work differs from the closest predecessor, \\citet{du2026geometric}, in four respects:\n(i)~we compare an entire family of canary metrics rather than a single metric pair;\n(ii)~we cross-validate each lead-lag relationship across five independent statistical methods, quantifying inter-method sensitivity via Cohen's $\\kappa$ and intra-method sensitivity via the Toda--Yamamoto augmentation order $d_{\\max}$;\n(iii)~we map detectability as a function of contamination regime rather than reporting a single aggregate result; and\n(iv)~we treat method-selection sensitivity itself as a primary finding, showing that it is the dominant source of variation in existing lead-lag conclusions.\n\n\\paragraph{Training dynamics monitoring.}\n\nA broader literature tracks within-training diagnostics such as loss of plasticity~\\cite{dohare2024plasticity,lyle2023plasticity}, effective representation rank, and gradient norm statistics as indicators of learning health.\nThese approaches monitor a single training run and detect pathologies (saturated units, rank collapse, unstable gradients) that emerge over optimization steps.\nOur work differs in a key structural way: we analyze \\emph{cross-generation} temporal relationships between aggregate statistics computed after each full training cycle, applying time-series causal methods to a sequence of $T{=}10$ generation-level observations rather than tracking step-level dynamics within a single run.\nThis distinction is methodologically consequential because the short, non-stationary series that arise from generational measurement require entirely different statistical tools (Granger causality, permutation tests, differencing diagnostics) than the long, dense traces available from step-level monitoring.\n\n\\paragraph{Early-warning signals and critical transitions.}\n\nThe theoretical foundation for canary-based prediction of system failure originates in ecology and climate science.\n\\citet{scheffer2009early} established that complex systems approaching tipping points exhibit generic early-warning signals---rising autocorrelation and variance due to critical slowing down---independent of system-specific mechanisms.\n\\citet{scheffer2012anticipating} extended this framework by combining empirical warning indicators with network-architecture analysis, arguing that structural and statistical signals together improve anticipatory power.\nCrucially, \\citet{boettiger2012limits} quantified the statistical detection limits of these signals, showing that early-warning power is often negligible in short time series---a constraint directly relevant to iterative training, where the number of generations (typically 5--20) is far smaller than typical ecological datasets.\n\\citet{truong2025entropy} connects entropy collapse to critical transitions, characterizing it as a first-order phase transition with universality classes that determine whether classical early-warning signals retain predictive power.\nThe model collapse literature has not yet imported these methodological tools: existing canary studies neither frame their claims in terms of detection power under finite samples nor test whether observed lead-lag patterns survive the short-series constraints that \\citet{boettiger2012limits} identified as fundamental.\n\n\\paragraph{Lead-lag methodology.}\n\nGranger causality~\\cite{granger1969investigating} provides the standard econometric framework for testing whether one time series improves prediction of another beyond its own history.\n\\citet{shojaie2022granger} review classical and modern extensions, including VAR-based tests, kernel methods, and neural approaches for nonlinear dependencies \\cite{marcinkevics2022neural}.\nA key practical issue is non-stationarity: standard Granger tests applied to integrated (unit-root) series produce inflated rejection rates---the spurious regression problem formalized by \\citet{granger1974spurious}, who demonstrated that regressions between independent random walks yield spuriously significant $t$-statistics, a hazard directly applicable to co-trending metrics in iterative training.\nThe Toda--Yamamoto procedure~\\cite{toda1995statistical} addresses this by augmenting the VAR lag order by the integration order $d_{\\max}$ and testing only the original lags, yielding valid asymptotic inference regardless of cointegration structure; however, this robustness comes at the cost of reduced degrees of freedom, a trade-off that becomes severe in the short series ($T{\\leq}20$) typical of iterative training studies.\nDespite wide use in economics and neuroscience, these methods are largely absent from the machine learning literature on iterative training.\nExisting canary studies rely on visual inspection or naive cross-correlation, which cannot distinguish genuine temporal precedence from shared non-stationary trends.\nOur framework applies five complementary methods---naive cross-correlation, differenced cross-correlation, threshold onset detection, permutation Granger causality, and Toda--Yamamoto Granger causality---and requires multi-method consensus before classifying a lead-lag relationship as robust, directly addressing the method-sensitivity problem that arises when any single test is taken in isolation.\nRecent work on causal method reliability reinforces this multi-method rationale.\n\\citet{ruiz2026causalaudit} propose Causal-Audit, a pre-screening framework that validates causal assumptions before method selection and achieves 62\\% false positive reduction; whereas their approach filters methods \\emph{ex ante}, our consensus framework validates conclusions \\emph{ex post} across methods already applied.\n\\citet{yi2026causalcompass} benchmark causal discovery robustness under eight misspecification scenarios and confirm that no single method dominates universally, directly supporting the multi-method rationale of our consensus framework.\nIndependently, \\citet{sahoo2026calibration} show that calibration metrics are sensitive to training-distribution perturbation beyond the iterative collapse setting, suggesting that ECE-based canary signals may be confounded by non-collapse sources of calibration drift.\n\\citet{falahati2026curated} prove that pluralistic curation of synthetic data can prevent iterative collapse entirely, implying that canary detection is most relevant in uncontrolled data pipelines where such curation is absent.\n\nIn summary, the model collapse literature has established the phenomenon and proposed monitoring metrics, while the econometric literature provides the statistical tools for temporal precedence testing.\nHowever, no prior work bridges these two bodies of literature with the methodological rigor that short, non-stationary time series demand: systematic multi-method cross-validation, differencing diagnostics, false discovery correction, and regime-dependent calibration.\nOur consensus framework fills this gap.\nPAPER_EOF",
  "account": "root"
}

methodological pitfalls (\\S\\ref{sec:pitfalls}), proposes a multi-method consensus framework to address them (\\S\\ref{sec:consensus}), and calibrates the framework's error rates using built-in experimental controls (\\S\\ref{sec:calibration}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Methodological Pitfalls in Lead-Lag Analysis}\n\\label{sec:pitfalls}\n\nWe identify three pitfalls that, individually or in combination, can produce incorrect conclusions about temporal precedence in iterative training systems.\nEach is documented below with representative data from our experiments; full results appear in \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}.\n\n\\paragraph{Pitfall 1: Method sensitivity.}\nApplied to the same data ($\\alpha{=}1.0$, 4~seeds, 16 canary$\\to$downstream pairs---4 candidate canaries $\\times$ 4 downstream metrics, including candidates later excluded per \\S\\ref{sec:case_study}), five lead-lag detection methods yield FDR significance rates ranging from 100\\% (naive cross-correlation) to 25\\% (Toda--Yamamoto Granger causality), with inter-method agreement of Cohen's $\\kappa{=}0.185$---poor agreement (\\S\\ref{sec:method_sensitivity}).\nThe spurious-signal rate---the fraction of pairs flagged by the most liberal method but not confirmed by the most conservative---is 75\\% across all five methods and 62.5\\% when excluding Toda--Yamamoto, whose low statistical power at $T{=}10$ inflates this rate.\nThis pitfall is dangerous because method choice alone can determine whether a lead-lag relationship is deemed present or absent.\nIn a literature where single-method analyses are the norm, the implicit assumption that method choice does not matter is demonstrably false.\n\n\\paragraph{Pitfall 2: Spurious regression under shared trends.}\nA single preprocessing decision---first-differencing to remove shared non-stationary trends~\\cite{granger1974spurious}---can eliminate an entire analysis channel.\nAt $\\alpha{=}1.0$, canary$\\to$perplexity pairs appear near-universally significant in undifferenced analysis ($F{=}16$--$36$) but yield $F{\\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$ after differencing (Figure~\\ref{fig:differencing_dmax}a; \\S\\ref{sec:method_sensitivity}).\nIn contrast, canary$\\to$diversity pairs retain unanimous FDR significance after differencing (24/24$\\to$24/24).\nWithout the differencing diagnostic, an analyst would conclude that canaries predict perplexity---a conclusion entirely driven by co-trending rather than temporal precedence.\nThe danger lies in the selective nature of this confound: only by applying the diagnostic can one determine which channels are genuine and which are artifacts of shared non-stationarity.\n\n\\paragraph{Pitfall 3: Regime dependence.}\nEven after fixing the statistical method, the contamination level~$\\alpha$ determines whether a lead-lag relationship is detectable.\nPermutation Granger tests (first-differenced, BH FDR-corrected at $q{=}0.05$) find 6/8 pairs FDR-significant at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ but 0/24 at $\\alpha{\\leq}0.25$ (three seeds), with seed-dependent results (0/8--6/8) at $\\alpha{=}0.50$ (\\S\\ref{sec:regime}).\nThe $\\alpha{=}0.50$ regime is particularly dangerous: correlation-based methods unanimously find significance while causal methods unanimously find non-significance (Figure~\\ref{fig:consensus_heatmap}), creating the maximum risk of spurious conclusions---high correlation coexists with zero causal precedence.\nThis regime dependence is consistent with theoretical expectations about signal-to-noise limits in short ecological time series \\cite{boettiger2012limits}, and suggests that detectability boundaries should be explicitly mapped rather than assumed.\n\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Method Consensus Framework}\n\\label{sec:consensus}\n\nThe pitfalls above motivate a consensus approach inspired by multi-method validation practices in early-warning signal detection \\cite{scheffer2009early}: rather than relying on any single method, we require agreement across multiple independent methods and random seeds before classifying a lead-lag relationship as robust.\n\n\\paragraph{Formal definition.}\nGiven $M$ lead-lag detection methods $\\{m_1, \\ldots, m_M\\}$ and $N$ independent random seeds, a canary$\\to$downstream pair $(X, Y)$ is classified as \\textsc{robust} if and only if:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item At least $K$ of $M$ methods judge the pair FDR-significant, where each method independently applies Benjamini--Hochberg correction at $q{=}0.05$; and\n    \\item At least $S$ of $N$ seeds replicate this conclusion.\n\\end{enumerate}\nThe thresholds $(K, S)$ control the trade-off between false positive and false negative rates.\nMethods with different inductive biases---correlation-based methods that detect any temporal association versus Granger-type methods that test directional predictive improvement \\cite{granger1969investigating}---provide complementary evidence.\nTheir convergence on a given pair constitutes stronger evidence than agreement among methods sharing the same statistical assumptions.\nWe note that Granger precedence establishes temporal predictive improvement, not true causality \\cite{shojaie2022granger}; our framework inherits this limitation.\n\n\\paragraph{Sequential filtering pipeline.}\nIn practice, the $K$-of-$M$ voting operates over all five methods applied independently to each pair; the sequential pipeline below describes the order in which results are examined, enabling early termination and interpretable diagnostics at each stage (Figure~\\ref{fig:consensus_workflow}):\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Naive correlation scan.} Cross-correlation at maximum lag~5 with BH correction serves as a permissive baseline; all pairs with any temporal association pass.\n    \\item \\textbf{First-differencing diagnostic.} Cross-correlation is recomputed on first-differenced series, testing whether temporal associations survive removal of shared linear trends. Pairs that lose significance are flagged as potential spurious regressions.\n    \\item \\textbf{Permutation Granger causality.} Permutation-based Granger causality tests \\cite{shojaie2022granger} on first-differenced series (5{,}000 permutations, BH correction at $q{=}0.05$) assess directional predictive improvement: whether past values of $X$ improve prediction of $Y$ beyond $Y$'s own history.\n    \\item \\textbf{Multi-seed replication.} The conclusion must hold across $S$ of $N$ independent training seeds, filtering seed-specific artifacts.\n\\end{enumerate}\nAt $\\alpha{=}1.0$, this pipeline progressively filters: 16 pairs pass Stage~1, 14 survive Stage~2 (differencing removes 2 marginal canary$\\to$perplexity pairs), 6 survive Stage~3 (Granger testing eliminates remaining canary$\\to$perplexity pairs and failed canary candidates), and all~6 survive Stage~4 (multi-seed replication confirms all Granger-significant pairs).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{figures/consensus_workflow.pdf}\n\\caption{Sequential filtering pipeline of the multi-method consensus framework. Starting from all candidate canary$\\to$downstream pairs, the pipeline applies four stages: (1)~naive cross-correlation scan (permissive baseline), (2)~first-differencing diagnostic (removes shared-trend artifacts), (3)~permutation Granger causality (tests directional predictive improvement), and (4)~multi-seed replication (filters seed-specific artifacts). At $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (Pythia-410M), the pipeline reduces 16 candidate pairs to 6 robust pairs: 16$\\to$14$\\to$6$\\to$6. The $K$-of-$M$ consensus vote (requiring $K{\\geq}4/5$ methods) operates in parallel across all five methods; the sequential ordering provides interpretable diagnostics at each stage.}\n\\label{fig:consensus_workflow}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Practical guidance.}\nThe consensus thresholds $(K, S)$ should be chosen to match the analyst's tolerance for false positives versus false negatives.\nTable~\\ref{tab:practical_guidance} provides four recommended tiers, calibrated against our experimental controls (\\S\\ref{sec:calibration}).\nTier~3 ($K{\\geq}4/5$, $S{\\geq}3/4$) achieves the best trade-off in our setting: zero false positives while recovering all six reference pairs.\nMethod subset ablation confirms that this threshold is uniquely optimal: at $K{=}3/5$, the null-control FPR is 37.5\\% (unacceptable); at $K{=}4/5$, FPR drops to 0\\% while retaining 6/6 reference-pair sensitivity; at $K{=}5/5$, FPR remains 0\\% but sensitivity falls to 3/6.\nShapley value analysis identifies permutation Granger causality as the linchpin method (Shapley $= +0.375$), the only method combining 0\\% null-control FPR with high sensitivity.\nToda--Yamamoto contributes negatively (Shapley $= -2.625$) due to its Type~II errors at $T{=}10$; we retain it as insurance against unknown integration order in our consensus panel, but note its severe power limitations at $T{\\leq}10$ and caution that its results should be interpreted accordingly.\nTier~4 requires unanimity across methods and seeds, which incurs a 50\\% false negative rate because Toda--Yamamoto produces Type~II errors at $T{=}10$ due to insufficient residual degrees of freedom (\\S\\ref{sec:consensus_validation}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Practical guidance tiers for lead-lag consensus analysis. Tier~3 ($K{\\geq}4/5$, $S{\\geq}3/4$) is uniquely optimal: it achieves FPR${=}$0\\% while retaining all 6 reference pairs (FNR${=}$0\\%). FPR is calibrated at $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (null control, $M{=}5$ methods, $N{=}3$ seeds, Pythia-410M). FNR is calibrated at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ against 6 effect-size reference pairs using a 4-method panel (excluding differenced cross-correlation); $N{=}4$ seeds (\\S\\ref{sec:calibration}).}\n\\label{tab:practical_guidance}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\nTier & Use Case & $K/M$ & $S/N$ & FPR & FNR \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 & Exploratory     & ${\\geq}1/5$ & ${\\geq}1/N$ & 100\\%  & 0\\%    \\\\\n2 & Screening       & ${\\geq}3/5$ & ${\\geq}2/N$ & 37.5\\% & 0\\%    \\\\\n3 & Confirmatory    & ${\\geq}4/5$ & ${\\geq}3/N$ & 0\\%    & 0\\%    \\\\\n4 & Conservative    & $5/5$       & $N/N$        & 0\\%    & 50\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Empirical Calibration}\n\\label{sec:calibration}\n\nWe calibrate the framework using two built-in experimental controls.\nFull numerical results are reported in Table~\\ref{tab:fpr_fnr}.\n\n\\paragraph{False positive rate.}\nThe $\\alpha{=}0.0$ condition serves as a null control: no synthetic data is injected, so any detected lead-lag association is a false positive.\nAt $K{=}1$ (any single method suffices), FPR${=}$100\\%---naive cross-correlation alone flags all pairs.\nAt $K{=}2$, FPR drops to 75\\%; at $K{=}3$, to 37.5\\%; and at $K{\\geq}4$, FPR${=}$0\\%.\nThe consensus framework at Tier~3 matches the false positive performance of the best individual methods (permutation Granger and Toda--Yamamoto, both 0\\%) while providing robustness against miscalibration of any single method.\n\n\\paragraph{False negative rate.}\nAt $\\alpha{=}1.0$ with 4~seeds, we define six \\emph{effect-size reference pairs} using criteria independent of the consensus methods:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item[(a)] The canary$\\to$downstream drift ratio at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ must exceed $3\\times$ the $\\alpha{=}0.0$ baseline drift (canary ratios: entropy $11.93\\times$, ECE $73.12\\times$---both far above threshold); and\n    \\item[(b)] Canary onset must strictly precede downstream onset by at least one generation (canary onset: generation~1; diversity onset: generations~3--9).\n\\end{enumerate}\nThese criteria yield token entropy and ECE each predicting distinct-1, distinct-2, and distinct-3~\\cite{li2016diversity}.\nThe perplexity channel is excluded because its onset (generation~1) coincides with canary onset, consistent with---but not derived from---the differencing diagnostic finding that canary$\\to$perplexity associations are spurious (\\S\\ref{sec:method_sensitivity}).\nThe effect-size threshold is robust: at $2\\times$, $3\\times$, and $5\\times$ the baseline drift, the reference set remains identical (6~pairs), because canary ratios ($11.93\\times$ and $73.12\\times$) far exceed all thresholds.\n\nFNR is calibrated using a 4-method panel excluding differenced cross-correlation, which shares statistical properties with detrending-based criteria and could introduce circularity.\nAt $K{\\geq}3/4$, $S{\\geq}3/4$, all six reference pairs are recovered (FNR${=}$0\\%).\nAt $K{=}4/4$, $S{=}4/4$, FNR rises to 50\\% because Toda--Yamamoto produces Type~II errors at $T{=}10$ due to insufficient residual degrees of freedom (\\S\\ref{sec:consensus_validation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Symmetric calibration.}\nBoth FPR and FNR are validated on the joint method${\\times}$seed dimension: FPR across 3~seeds at $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (0/24 pairs at $K{\\geq}4/5$) and FNR across 4~seeds at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (6/6 reference pairs recovered at $K{\\geq}3/4$, $S{\\geq}3/4$).\nCross-seed FPR variance is negligible: all three null-control seeds yield 0/8 robust pairs at the consensus threshold, confirming seed-independent performance.\nPAPER_EOF",

causation.}\nGranger causality tests whether past values of one series improve prediction of another beyond its own history; it does not establish true causation~\\cite{granger1969investigating,shojaie2022granger}.\nBivariate Granger tests cannot exclude confounders: an unobserved process---such as a latent distributional shift in the training data---could independently drive both token entropy and diversity metrics, producing spurious lead-lag associations even when no direct relationship exists.\nOur multi-method consensus framework inherits this limitation in full.\nThroughout this paper, ``Granger precedence'' denotes predictive improvement in a time-series forecasting sense, not a causal mechanism.\nThis distinction is why we scope our central claim to ``token entropy Granger-precedes diversity collapse'' rather than asserting that token entropy \\emph{causes} diversity collapse.\n\n\\paragraph{Calibration scope and generalization.}\nAll FPR and FNR calibration results reported in this work are specific to our experimental setup: Pythia-410M~\\cite{biderman2023pythia}, a WikiText-103 subset, $T{=}10$ generation cycles, and five contamination levels ($\\alpha \\in \\{0.0, 0.25, 0.50, 0.75, 1.0\\}$).\nGeneralization to other model architectures, datasets, or training protocols requires independent validation.\nThe consensus framework's structure---multi-method agreement combined with multi-seed replication---is intended to be applicable beyond this specific setting, but the operating thresholds confirming that the detectability boundary is consistent across two transformer decoder-only model families of similar scale.\nThe full GPT-2-medium drift profile at $\\alpha{=}0.25$ is: entropy method${\\times}$seed dimension: FPR uses three seeds at the null-control condition ($\\alpha{=}0.0$, 0/24 pairs at $K{\\geq}4/5$) and FNR aggregates across four seeds at full contamination operate near the lower bound of what standard methods require.\nThis series length is typical for iterative training studies, where each generation involves a complete training run, but it severely limits statistical power.\nThe Toda--Yamamoto procedure's elevated FNR (33.3\\%, missing 2 of 6 directional pairs) is attributable to insufficient residual degrees of freedom at this series length, not to a fundamental deficiency in the method itself.\nThis finding aligns with \\citet{boettiger2012limits}, who demonstrated that early-warning signals have limited detection power in short time series.\nLonger generation chains would improve power for all methods but impose substantial computational cost; this trade-off between statistical power and compute budget is inherent to iterative training research and cannot be resolved by methodological improvements alone.\nHowever, observation window sensitivity analysis provides reassurance: the $4/5$ consensus classification stabilizes at $T{\\geq}5$, well below our observation window of $T{=}10$, with identical results from $T{=}5$ through $T{=}10$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:window_sensitivity}).\nThe Toda--Yamamoto procedure produces valid test statistics at all window lengths ($T \\in \\{5, \\ldots, 10\\}$), but with unstable sensitivity---3/6 to 6/6 pairs reach significance depending on window length---confirming that its elevated FNR reflects limited residual degrees of freedom rather than absence of signal.\n\n\\paragraph{Effective rank direction is model-dependent.}\nEffective rank evolves in opposite directions across architectures during iterative training.\nIn Pythia-410M, effective rank decreases (representational contraction), consistent with mode collapse concentrating the output distribution into fewer dimensions.\nIn GPT-2-medium at $\\alpha{=}0.25$, effective rank \\emph{increases} from 2.37 to 8.48 (+258\\%).\nThe likely explanation is starting-point dependence: GPT-2-medium begins near rank-2 (a highly concentrated initial representation), and iterative training disperses activation mass across more dimensions rather than concentrating it further.\nThis finding demonstrates that effective rank cannot serve as a direction-agnostic canary and that its trajectory must be interpreted relative to architecture-specific baselines.\nWe note this as a caveat on the generality of representational metrics as collapse indicators, even when the regime map (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_arch_regime}) and lead-lag detectability are consistent across architectures.\n\n\\paragraph{Diversity--calibration dissociation.}\nDiversity metrics and calibration metrics respond to synthetic contamination in qualitatively opposite directions across the $\\alpha$ spectrum.\nDiversity collapse (distinct-$n$ decline) is most severe at low contamination ($\\alpha{=}0.25$: distinct-3 declines by $58.9{\\pm}0.9\\%$) and attenuates at higher levels ($\\alpha{=}1.0$: $-30.5\\%$), while calibration degradation (ECE) increases monotonically with $\\alpha$ ($+51.7\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.0$ to $+3780\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}1.0$).\nThis dissociation underscores the need for multi-method consensus: the regime in which a metric degrades most severely ($\\alpha{=}0.25$ for diversity) does not coincide with the regime in which its temporal lead is most detectable ($\\alpha{\\geq}0.75$), and a single metric family cannot capture the full picture of iterative training degradation.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical utility paradox.}\nThe canary framework exhibits a paradox: it works best where it is least needed.\nAt $\\alpha{\\geq}0.75$, token entropy reliably Granger-precedes diversity collapse---but at these contamination levels, degradation is already severe and likely detectable through simpler means.\nAt $\\alpha{\\leq}0.25$, where early warning would be most valuable, the canary signal is undetectable by any method tested; at $\\alpha{=}0.50$, detectability is seed-dependent (0/8--6/8, 3~seeds, median 3/8), marking the onset boundary where ECE outperforms token entropy as the more sensitive canary.\nThis is not a framework deficiency but an inherent characteristic of collapse dynamics: at low contamination, diversity changes are too small relative to inter-generation noise for any temporal lead to emerge above the statistical detection floor~\\cite{boettiger2012limits}.\nThe practical implication is that the framework is better suited to continuous monitoring dashboards---tracking cumulative trend shifts across many generations---than to single-point threshold alarms.\nIf contamination grows over time (as in realistic web-scale data pipelines), the canary signal will strengthen as $\\alpha$ increases, providing earlier detection than post-hoc quality audits.\n\n\\paragraph{Token entropy versus perplexity.}\nThe finding that token entropy Granger-precedes diversity while perplexity does not ($F{\\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$ after differencing) requires clarification, since perplexity is the exponential of cross-entropy and appears mathematically related to token entropy.\nThe distinction lies in the evaluation domain: our ``token entropy'' is $H(p_\\theta | \\text{context})$ evaluated on \\emph{held-out real data}---measuring the model's predictive uncertainty on genuine text---while ``perplexity'' is $\\exp(H_\\text{cross})$ evaluated on \\emph{model-generated text}---measuring the model's self-consistency on its own outputs.\nThe identity $\\text{ppl} = \\exp(\\text{entropy})$ holds only when the model distribution equals the data distribution, a condition that iterative collapse systematically violates.\nIn practice, both metrics trend downward during collapse (the model becomes more certain), producing high undifferenced correlation.\nFirst-differencing removes this shared trend: the canary$\\to$diversity channel survives because entropy \\emph{leads} diversity decline by one generation (the model narrows its distribution before output diversity measurably falls), whereas the canary$\\to$perplexity channel vanishes because entropy and perplexity co-move \\emph{synchronously}---both driven by the same underlying distributional contraction.\nThus, the perplexity channel being ``spurious'' is not trivially expected from the mathematical relationship, but rather a substantive finding about collapse dynamics: the generation-evaluation divergence breaks the entropy--perplexity coupling in a temporally informative way.\n\n\\paragraph{NLP-specific constraints on lead-lag analysis.}\nOur application of Granger-type methods to iterative training faces constraints absent in their original econometric settings.\nFirst, observation length: typical econometric applications use $T{=}100$--$1000$ time points, whereas iterative training studies are limited to $T{=}5$--$20$ generations due to computational cost.\nThis severely constrains degrees of freedom for VAR-based methods and explains the Toda--Yamamoto procedure's poor power in our setting.\nSecond, non-stationarity is by design: each generation's training data differs from the previous generation's, making the underlying process non-stationary by construction rather than as an incidental property.\nStandard stationarity tests (ADF, KPSS) may yield misleading results when applied to processes with deterministic degradation trajectories rather than stochastic unit roots.\nThird, the sample-size bottleneck compounds with multiple testing: with only 8--32 pairs per regime, FDR correction operates on small multiplicity, limiting its ability to separate true from false discoveries.\nThese constraints motivate our multi-method consensus approach as a complement to---rather than replacement for---traditional significance testing.\n\n\\paragraph{Future work.}\nSeveral directions extend this work.\nMultivariate VAR models would enable testing for confounders and multi-directional Granger relationships beyond our current bivariate framework.\nCross-architecture validation beyond our single-seed GPT-2-medium validation requires multi-seed replication; evaluation on larger-scale models would further test generalizability.\nOnline monitoring---integrating canary metrics into training pipelines for real-time collapse detection---is a natural applied extension.\nAccumulate-mode experiments~\\cite{dey2024breaking} would test whether canary dynamics differ from the replace-mode setting studied here.\nFinally, connecting the empirically observed onset boundary at $\\alpha{\\approx}0.25$--$0.50$ to theoretical phase transition analysis~\\cite{truong2025entropy} could yield a principled explanation for why canary signals emerge at this contamination regime rather than at lower contamination levels.\nPAPER_EOF",
  "account": "root"
}

  "command": "cat << 'PAPER_EOF' > /root/autodl-tmp/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/experiments.tex\n\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\nWe study lead-lag analysis pitfalls in iterative training collapse using Pythia-410M \\cite{biderman2023pythia}, a 410M-parameter decoder-only language model.\nPythia provides a controlled setting: small enough for multi-seed experiments across five contamination levels, yet large enough to exhibit meaningful generation-over-generation degradation.\nCross-architecture validation uses GPT-2-medium (345M parameters, also decoder-only), a second transformer family of similar scale; results are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:method_sensitivity}, \\S\\ref{sec:regime}, and \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}.\nTraining data is drawn from WikiText-103 \\cite{merity2017pointer}, using 5{,}000-sample subsets per generation.\n\nOur protocol follows the replace paradigm of \\citet{shumailov2024curse}, adapted for mixed synthetic-real training at $\\alpha{<}1.0$.\nEach generation $t$ proceeds in three steps: (1)~fine-tune the generation-$(t{-}1)$ model for 5{,}000 steps on a mixture of $\\alpha$ fraction synthetic data and $(1{-}\\alpha)$ fraction real data (AdamW optimizer, learning rate $5 \\times 10^{-5}$, 500 warmup steps, weight decay $0.01$, batch size 4 for Pythia-410M, 32 for GPT-2-medium); (2)~generate 5{,}000 synthetic samples from the fine-tuned model (temperature $1.0$, top-$k{=}50$, max length 512 tokens); (3)~fully replace the synthetic pool.\nWe run $T{=}10$ generations (11 time points including the generation-0 baseline) across contamination levels $\\alpha \\in \\{0.00, 0.25, 0.50, 0.75, 1.00\\}$.\nThe $\\alpha{=}1.0$ condition uses four random seeds $\\{42, 43, 44, 45\\}$; $\\alpha{=}0.00$, $\\alpha{=}0.25$, and $\\alpha{=}0.75$ each use three seeds $\\{42, 43, 44\\}$; $\\alpha{=}0.50$ uses three seeds $\\{42, 43, 44\\}$.\n\nWe track two \\emph{canary metrics}---cheap-to-compute statistics hypothesized to precede downstream degradation, inspired by early-warning signals in dynamical systems \\cite{scheffer2009early}---and three \\emph{downstream metrics}.\nCanary metrics are token entropy (next-token prediction entropy averaged over held-out samples) and expected calibration error (ECE).\nDownstream metrics are distinct-1 and distinct-2 ($n$-gram diversity \\cite{li2016diversity}, computed over all 5{,}000 generated samples per generation) and perplexity (evaluated on held-out real data).\nToken entropy is the mean next-token prediction entropy $H(p_\\theta | \\text{context})$ averaged over 1{,}000 held-out samples; ECE uses 15 equal-width bins.\nWe additionally evaluate effective rank and Self-BLEU as candidate canaries (\\S\\ref{sec:case_study}).\nResults for distinct-3 are reported in Appendix~\\ref{app:distinct3}.\nCode and data will be released upon publication.\n\nAll hypothesis tests apply Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) FDR correction at $q{=}0.05$.\nPermutation Granger causality tests use 5{,}000 permutations on first-differenced series \\cite{shojaie2022granger}.\nCross-correlation analysis uses a maximum lag of 5 generations.\nTable~\\ref{tab:drift_summary} summarizes raw metric drift from generation~0 to generation~10 across all five contamination levels.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Metric drift (\\%, generation~0$\\to$10) for Pythia-410M across contamination levels. ($\\alpha{=}1.0$: $-27.8{\\pm}1.6\\%$). $\\alpha{=}0.00$, $\\alpha{=}0.25$, $\\alpha{=}0.75$: 3-seed mean${\\pm}$std (seeds 42, 43, 44); $\\alpha{=}1.0$: 4-seed mean${\\pm}$std (seeds 42, 43, 44, 45); $\\alpha{=}0.50$: seed~42.}\n\\label{tab:drift_summary}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\alpha$ & Entropy & ECE & Distinct-1 & Distinct-2 & Perplexity \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.00 & $-4.6{\\pm}0.4$ & $+37.6{\\pm}14$ & $-14.1{\\pm}0.4$ & $-9.6{\\pm}0.4$ & $-20.6{\\pm}1.0$ \\\\\n0.25 & $-4.7{\\pm}0.5$ & $+81.8{\\pm}15.9$ & $-40.3{\\pm}0.3$ & $-56.4{\\pm}0.7$ & $-18.1{\\pm}0.8$ \\\\\n0.50 & $-18.6$ & $+842.8$ & $-37.3$ & $-51.5$ & $+21.1$ \\\\\n0.75 & $-27.6{\\pm}0.2$ & $+1791{\\pm}18$ & $-32.3{\\pm}0.5$ & $-41.0{\\pm}1.0$ & $+182.6{\\pm}0.7$ \\\\\n1.00 & $-60.8{\\pm}1.2$ & $+3780{\\pm}30$ & $-27.8{\\pm}1.6$ & $-34.0{\\pm}3.3$ & $+3674{\\pm}45$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Method Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{sec:method_sensitivity}\n\nWe open with Claim~3---method sensitivity---because it conditions the interpretation of all subsequent results.\nIf different statistical methods yield contradictory conclusions on the same data, any single-method analysis is unreliable by construction.\n\n\\paragraph{Five-method ablation.}\nWe apply five lead-lag detection methods to the $\\alpha{=}1.0$ condition (4~seeds, 16 canary$\\to$downstream pairs).\nThe fraction of pairs reaching BH-corrected FDR significance varies dramatically by method: naive cross-correlation \\textbf{16/16} (100\\%), differenced cross-correlation \\textbf{14/16} (87.5\\%), threshold onset detection \\textbf{9/16} (56.3\\%), permutation Granger causality \\textbf{6/16} (37.5\\%), and Toda--Yamamoto Granger \\textbf{4/16} (25.0\\%).\nMethod choice alone swings significance from \\textbf{100\\% to 25\\%}: 50--57\\% of pairs classified as significant under one method flip to non-significant under another.\nThe spurious-signal rate---the fraction of pairs detected by the most liberal method but not confirmed by the most conservative---is \\textbf{75\\%} across all five methods and \\textbf{62.5\\%} when excluding Toda--Yamamoto, whose low statistical power at $T{=}10$ inflates this rate (\\S\\ref{sec:consensus_validation}).\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} reports significance rates restricted to 8 validated canary$\\to$downstream pairs (excluding failed candidates); the 16-pair rates above include effective rank and Self-BLEU to capture the full method-sensitivity landscape.\n\n\\paragraph{First-differencing diagnostic.}\nA single preprocessing decision can eliminate an entire analysis channel.\nFor canary$\\to$diversity pairs at $\\alpha{=}1.0$, all pairs retain unanimous FDR significance after first-differencing (24/24$\\to$24/24), confirming genuine temporal precedence rather than shared-trend confounding.\nFor canary$\\to$perplexity, however, undifferenced tests find near-universal significance ($F{=}16$--$36$) while differenced tests find \\textbf{zero} significant pairs ($F{\\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$; Figure~\\ref{fig:differencing_dmax}a).\nThe apparent canary$\\to$perplexity relationship is entirely an artifact of co-trending.\nNotably, at $\\alpha{=}0.75$, differenced cross-correlation finds 8/8 pairs significant in \\emph{both} forward and reverse directions per seed, demonstrating that this method alone cannot distinguish genuine directional precedence from shared dynamics even after detrending.\n\n\\paragraph{Augmentation order sensitivity.}\nToda--Yamamoto Granger tests show extreme sensitivity to the augmentation order $d_{\\max}$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:differencing_dmax}b).\nAt $d_{\\max}{=}0$ (most permissive), approximately 6\\% of pairs are rejected; at adaptive $d_{\\max}$ determined by ADF unit-root tests, 75\\%; at $d_{\\max}{=}1$ (most conservative), \\textbf{94\\%}.\nThis single hyperparameter swings conclusions from near-universal significance to near-universal non-significance.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{figures/differencing_dmax.pdf}\n\\caption{First-differencing diagnostic and augmentation order sensitivity (Pythia-410M, $\\alpha{=}1.0$, 4~seeds). \\textbf{(a)}~First-differencing eliminates the canary$\\to$perplexity channel: undifferenced tests find near-universal significance ($F{=}16$--$36$) while differenced tests find zero significant pairs ($F{\\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$), exposing these associations as shared-trend artifacts. Canary$\\to$diversity pairs retain significance after differencing (24/24$\\to$24/24). \\textbf{(b)}~Toda--Yamamoto Granger significance rates vary dramatically with augmentation order $d_{\\max}$: from $\\sim$6\\% rejection at $d_{\\max}{=}0$ to 94\\% at $d_{\\max}{=}1$, demonstrating that this single hyperparameter can reverse conclusions about temporal precedence.}\n\\label{fig:differencing_dmax}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Inter-method agreement.}\nCohen's $\\kappa$, averaged over all method pairs, is \\textbf{0.185} (near chance; poor agreement); this value is consistent across contamination levels ($\\alpha{=}0.0$ through $\\alpha{=}1.0$), indicating that method disagreement is a structural property of the analysis rather than a regime-specific artifact.\nCross-architecture validation on GPT-2-medium (12 canary$\\to$downstream pairs) yields Fleiss' $\\kappa{=}0.091$, also in the ``slight agreement'' range, confirming that low inter-method agreement is not architecture-specific.\nRestricting to three informative methods (excluding naive cross-correlation and Toda--Yamamoto, which anchor the permissive and conservative extremes respectively) raises $\\kappa$ to 0.667 (substantial agreement), indicating that the low full-panel $\\kappa$ is driven by the deliberate inclusion of methods spanning the entire permissive-to-conservative spectrum rather than by fundamental incoherence among methods.\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} extends the analysis across contamination levels.\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (no synthetic data, serving as null control), naive cross-correlation finds \\textbf{100\\%} of pairs significant---a textbook spurious-regression outcome in trending time series.\nPermutation Granger correctly finds 0\\% across all three null-control seeds; Toda--Yamamoto finds 0--12.5\\% (1/24 total, attributable to a degenerate $\\text{df}_{\\text{resid}}{=}1$ case; \\S\\ref{sec:consensus_validation}).\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.50$, correlation-based methods (naive, differenced, threshold) unanimously find significance while causal methods unanimously find non-significance (Figure~\\ref{fig:consensus_heatmap}), the most extreme method$\\times$regime divergence in our experiments.\n\n\\paragraph{Method$\\times$regime interaction.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} reveals that the correlation-versus-causal divergence is not uniform across contamination levels.\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.25$, differenced cross-correlation drops to 25\\% and threshold onset to 37.5\\%, while naive cross-correlation remains at 100\\%---demonstrating that even partial detrending removes marginal associations at low contamination.\nIn the lowest-contamination regime ($\\alpha{\\leq}0.25$, three seeds), the false confirmation rate for the most liberal method approaches 100\\%, while all Granger-type tests correctly find 0\\%---a regime where single-method analysis is maximally dangerous.\nThe graded response across methods, combined with the block structure visible in Figure~\\ref{fig:consensus_heatmap}, motivates the regime-dependent analysis in \\S\\ref{sec:regime}.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{figures/consensus_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Method$\\times$regime consensus heatmap (Pythia-410M). Each cell shows the consensus tier ($K$-of-$M$ agreement) for a given lead-lag method (rows) and contamination level $\\alpha$ (columns). A clear block structure emerges: correlation-based methods (naive, differenced, threshold) find significance across all $\\alpha$ levels, while Granger-type methods find significance only at $\\alpha{\\geq}0.75$. The $\\alpha{=}0.50$ column is maximally dangerous---high correlation coexists with zero causal precedence---illustrating Pitfall~3 (regime dependence). Color scale: dark = significant (Tier~3), light = non-significant (Tier~1).}\n\\label{fig:consensus_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{FDR significance rates (\\%) for five lead-lag methods across contamination levels (Pythia-410M). Method choice swings significance from 100\\% (naive cross-correlation) to 0\\% (permutation Granger) at $\\alpha{\\leq}0.25$, with seed-dependent onset at $\\alpha{=}0.50$, demonstrating that single-method conclusions are unreliable. Each cell reports the fraction of 8 canary$\\to$downstream pairs (token entropy and ECE $\\times$ 4 downstream metrics) reaching BH-corrected significance ($q{=}0.05$), excluding failed candidates (\\S\\ref{sec:case_study}). Seed coverage: $\\alpha{=}1.0$: 4 seeds (32 pairs); $\\alpha{=}0.00$, $\\alpha{=}0.75$: 3 seeds (24 pairs); $\\alpha{=}0.25$ Toda--Yamamoto: 3 seeds (24 pairs), other methods single seed; $\\alpha{=}0.50$: 3~seeds for permutation Granger (range shown), single seed for other methods. $^\\dagger$Seed-dependent onset: s42${=}$0/8, s43${=}$6/8, s44${=}$3/8 (median \\\\\n\\midrule\nNaive XCorr     & 100   & 100  & 100  & 100   & 100   & 100  \\\\\nDiff.\\ XCorr   & 37.5  & 25   & 100  & 100   & 100   & 37.5 \\\\\nThreshold       & 62.5  & 37.5 & 100  & 50    & 100   & 62.5 \\\\\nPerm.\\ Granger  & 0     & 0    & 0--75$^\\dagger$  & 79.2  & 75    & 0    \\\\\nToda--Yamamoto  & 0     & 12.5 & 0    & 12.5  & 59.4  & 0    \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Regime-Dependent Detectability}\n\\label{sec:regime}\n\nBeyond method choice, the contamination level~$\\alpha$ determines whether a temporal lead exists to detect.\nWe present regime effects at two levels: raw metric drift (method-independent) and Granger-precedence detectability.\n\n\\paragraph{Raw drift.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:drift_summary} shows that diversity collapse occurs at \\emph{all} contamination levels, not only under full synthetic replacement.\nDistinct-1 declines by 13.8\\% even at $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (attributable to fine-tuning on repeated real-data subsets), and more steeply at intermediate contamination: $-40.3{\\pm}0.3\\%$ ($\\alpha{=}0.25$), $-37.3\\%$ ($\\alpha{=}0.50$), $-32.3{\\pm}0.5\\%$ ($\\alpha{=}0.75$), and $-27.8{\\pm}1.6\\%$ ($\\alpha{=}1.0$).\nThe non-monotonic pattern---diversity declining \\emph{more} at moderate~$\\alpha$ than at extreme~$\\alpha$---is confirmed by 3-seed replication at $\\alpha{=}0.25$ (std${=}0.3\\%$), indicating that this relationship between contamination level and collapse severity is robust rather than a seed-specific artifact.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{figures/regime_map.pdf}\n\\caption{Detectability regime map across contamination levels (Pythia-410M). Permutation Granger FDR significance rate (solid line) and consensus tier (shaded regions) as a function of $\\alpha$. The onset boundary lies at $\\alpha{\\approx}0.25$--$0.50$: at $\\alpha{\\leq}0.25$, all seeds agree on null (0/24); at $\\alpha{=}0.50$, detectability is seed-dependent (0/8--6/8, 3~seeds, median 3/8); at $\\alpha{=}0.75$, directional asymmetry emerges robustly (19/24 forward, 0/24 reverse, 3~seeds) but method consensus remains transitional (Tier~2--3); at $\\alpha{=}1.0$, full Confirmatory consensus is achieved (24/24 forward, Tier~3, 4~seeds). Multi-seed replication: $\\alpha{=}0.00$, $\\alpha{=}0.25$, $\\alpha{=}0.50$, $\\alpha{=}0.75$: 3~seeds; $\\alpha{=}1.0$: 4~seeds.}\n\\label{fig:regime_map}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Granger-precedence detectability.}\nDespite ubiquitous drift, causal detectability is sharply regime-dependent (Figure~\\ref{fig:regime_map}).\nPermutation Granger tests (first-differenced, BH-corrected) find 0/24 pairs FDR-significant at $\\alpha{=}0.00$ (3~seeds), 0/24 at $\\alpha{=}0.25$ (3~seeds), 0/8--6/8 at $\\alpha{=}0.50$ (seed-dependent; 3~seeds, median 3/8), and \\textbf{6/8} at $\\alpha{=}1.0$.\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.75$ (3~seeds), permutation Granger finds 19/24 forward-direction pairs FDR-significant with 0/24 reverse, confirming directional asymmetry; however, method-level consensus varies by seed (Tier~3 pairs: 4, 3, and 1 across seeds), placing this regime in a transitional detection state.\nUnder Bonferroni correction, approximately 3/8 pairs per seed survive---consistent with the transitional nature of the $\\alpha{=}0.75$ boundary where marginal signals are sensitive to correction stringency.\nWithin-$\\alpha$ multi-seed analysis at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ yields \\textbf{24/24} canary$\\to$diversity pairs FDR-significant across all four seeds, with 0/24 in the reverse direction, confirming temporal asymmetry.\nThe resulting dose-response pattern is monotonic: causal detectability is absent below $\\alpha{=}0.25$, seed-dependent at $\\alpha{=}0.50$, and robust above $\\alpha{=}0.75$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:regime_map}).\nThe onset boundary lies at $\\alpha{\\approx}0.25$--$0.50$, consistent with theoretical expectations about signal-to-noise limits in short time series~\\cite{boettiger2012limits}.\nAt the extremes, the transition is unambiguous: 0/24 FDR-significant pairs at $\\alpha{\\leq}0.25$ (3~seeds) versus 24/24 at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (4~seeds, Tier~3 consensus).\nBetween these extremes, individual-seed stochasticity determines whether the consensus threshold is crossed; the data map a boundary region rather than a sharp phase transition.\nSensitivity analysis confirms that treatment/null separation is robust to FDR threshold choice ($q{=}0.01$: 0/24 null, 0/24 treatment at $\\alpha{=}0.75$; $q{=}0.10$: 0/24 null, 24/24 treatment; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}).\nCross-architecture validation on GPT-2-medium confirms this boundary: at $\\alpha{=}0.25$, at most 1/8 pairs reach Confirmatory consensus ($K{\\geq}4/5$)---consistent with the null regime---despite substantially stronger canary drift (entropy $-32.8\\%$ vs.\\ Pythia $-4.7{\\pm}0.5\\%$), while at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ all 6 canary$\\to$diversity pairs reach $K{\\geq}4/5$ (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_arch_regime} summarizes the regime map across both architectures.\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.25$, GPT-2-medium yields 1/8 pairs at Tier~3 ($K{\\geq}4/5$) and 7/8 at Tier~2 ($K{\\geq}3/5$)---consistent with Pythia's 0/8 Tier~3, 0/8 Tier~2 result at the same contamination level, placing both architectures in the null/low-detectability regime.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-architecture regime map: lead-lag consensus tier and metric drift at $\\alpha{=}0.25$ and $\\alpha{=}1.0$. Tier~3: $K{\\geq}4/5$ methods; Tier~2: $K{\\geq}3/5$. Drift is generation~0$\\to$10. GPT-2-medium $\\alpha{=}0.25$: single seed (seed=42); Pythia $\\alpha{=}0.25$: 3-seed mean${\\pm}$std. The onset boundary ($\\alpha{\\approx}0.25$--$0.50$) is consistent across two transformer decoder-only model families of similar scale.}\n\\label{tab:cross_arch_regime}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\alpha$ & Model & Entropy & ECE & Distinct-1 & Distinct-2 & Perm.\\ Granger & Tier~3 \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.25 & Pythia-410M  & $-4.7{\\pm}0.5$ & $+81.8{\\pm}15.9$ & $-40.3{\\pm}0.3$ & $-56.4{\\pm}0.7$ & 0/8 & 0/8 \\\\\n0.25 & GPT-2-medium & $-32.8$ & $+422$ & $-23.5$ & $-31.6$ & 0/8 & 1/8 \\\\\n\\midrule\n1.00 & Pythia-410M  & $-60.8{\\pm}1.2$ & $+3780{\\pm}30$ & $-27.8{\\pm}1.6$ & $-34.0{\\pm}3.3$ & 6/8 & 6/6 \\\\\n1.00 & GPT-2-medium & \\multicolumn{6}{c}{(reported in \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}; all 6 canary$\\to$diversity Tier~3)} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Onset boundary at $\\alpha{=}0.50$.}\nThe $\\alpha{=}0.50$ regime provides particularly informative evidence.\nCorrelation-based methods unanimously find significance (Table~\\ref{tab:method_comparison}), and causal methods confirm emerging detectability in a majority of seeds: two of three seeds show directional asymmetry under permutation Granger (6/8 and 3/8 forward, 0/8 reverse), while one seed shows synchronous collapse (0/8).\nThe majority-signal outcome places $\\alpha{=}0.50$ in a transitional regime where causal detectability is emerging but not yet robust, bridging the null regime ($\\alpha{\\leq}0.25$) and the consistently detectable regime ($\\alpha{\\geq}0.75$).\nThis seed dependence exemplifies our central argument: even within a single regime and method, stochastic variation in training determines whether the consensus threshold is crossed.\nAt this boundary, canary sensitivity is metric-dependent: token entropy fails to reach FDR significance in any seed ($p_{\\text{fdr}}{\\geq}0.086$), while ECE achieves significance in the two signal-positive seeds (s43, s44), identifying ECE as the more sensitive canary at the onset boundary.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:consensus_heatmap} visualizes this interaction across all method$\\times\\alpha$ combinations, revealing a clear block structure that separates correlation-based methods from causal methods as a function of regime.\n\n\n\\subsection{Case Study: Canary Prediction of Diversity Collapse}\n\\label{sec:case_study}\n\nHaving established method sensitivity (Claim~3) and regime dependence (Claim~2), we present the conditions under which canary prediction succeeds (Claim~1).\nWe scope this claim precisely: canary metrics predict \\emph{diversity collapse} (declining distinct-$n$), not model collapse broadly.\n\n\\paragraph{Full synthetic replacement ($\\alpha{=}1.0$, 4 seeds).}\nToken entropy exhibits robust Granger-precedence over diversity collapse metrics, confirmed by 4 independent statistical methods (permutation Granger power${=}1.0$), 4 seeds, and cross-architecture replication on GPT-2-medium (Tier~3 consensus; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nCross-correlation peaks at lag$={+}1$ with $|r|{>}0.99$ (cross-seed standard deviation: 0.0), establishing a consistent one-generation lead.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:metric_trajectory} illustrates the one-generation offset between token entropy decline and distinct-1 decline, visible across all four seeds.\nPermutation Granger tests (first-differenced) confirm all canary$\\to$diversity pairs as FDR-significant across all four seeds, including distinct-3 (Appendix~\\ref{app:distinct3}).\nReverse-direction tests (downstream$\\to$canary) find \\textbf{0/24} FDR-significant (all $p{>}0.49$), establishing complete directional asymmetry.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{figures/metric_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Metric trajectories across 10 generations at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (Pythia-410M, 4~seeds shown as individual traces). Token entropy (top, blue) drops sharply at generation~1, while distinct-1 (bottom, red) begins declining at generations~2--3, establishing a visible one-generation canary lead. The consistent lag across all four seeds confirms that the temporal offset is robust rather than seed-specific. Shaded bands show the inter-seed range. This visual pattern corresponds to the statistical finding of 24/24 forward-direction and 0/24 reverse-direction FDR-significant pairs.}\n\\label{fig:metric_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{High contamination ($\\alpha{=}0.75$, 3 seeds).}\nDirectional asymmetry is robustly confirmed across three independent seeds (permutation Granger: 19/24 forward pairs FDR-significant, 0/24 reverse).\nHowever, method-level consensus varies by seed (Tier~3 pairs: 4, 3, and 1 across seeds 42, 43, 44), placing $\\alpha{=}0.75$ squarely in the transitional detection regime.\nDifferenced cross-correlation finds 8/8 pairs significant in \\emph{both} forward and reverse directions at each seed, confirming that this method alone cannot distinguish genuine directional precedence from shared dynamics---a seed-level demonstration of Pitfall~1 (\\S\\ref{sec:pitfalls}).\n\n\\paragraph{ECE as a conditional secondary canary.}\nECE performs equivalently to token entropy across four of five methods (naive, differenced, threshold, permutation Granger).\nIts classification as ``secondary'' is driven entirely by Toda--Yamamoto Type~II errors at $T{=}10$, where bootstrap power analysis yields estimated power of 0.05--0.20 depending on $d_{\\max}$.\nResolving ECE's true canary status requires longer time series.\n\n\\paragraph{Failed canary candidates.}\nNot all metrics serve as canaries.\nEffective rank and Self-BLEU show no detectable lead-lag relationship with any downstream metric under any method or contamination level, and are excluded from the consensus framework.\nEffective rank behavior is model-dependent in an unexpected way: in Pythia-410M, effective rank decreases during collapse (representational contraction), whereas in GPT-2-medium at $\\alpha{=}0.25$ it \\emph{increases} from 2.37 to 8.48 (+258\\%).\nThe GPT-2-medium starting effective rank is near rank-2, suggesting the model's initial representation is already highly concentrated; iterative training disperses it rather than concentrating it further.\nThis opposite directionality across architectures precludes effective rank as a universal canary and motivates architecture-specific analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\paragraph{Consensus filtering.}\nOf 16 canary$\\to$downstream pairs tested at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (4 candidate canaries $\\times$ 4 downstream metrics), \\textbf{6 survive} the full consensus pipeline ($K{\\geq}4/5$ methods, $S{\\geq}3/4$ seeds): token entropy and ECE each predicting distinct-1 and distinct-2 (4 pairs; distinct-3 results in Appendix~\\ref{app:distinct3} bring the total to~6).\nThe 10 eliminated pairs comprise all canary$\\to$perplexity relationships (identified as spurious by the first-differencing diagnostic) and all effective-rank and Self-BLEU pairs (failed canaries).\nThe consensus framework reduces the naive 100\\% significance rate to the 37.5\\% rate supported by the most conservative individual methods (Figure~\\ref{fig:consensus_workflow}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Consensus Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:consensus_validation}\n\nWe validate the consensus framework's calibration using built-in experimental controls.\n\n\\paragraph{False positive rate.}\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (three seeds, 24 total pairs), no synthetic data is injected; any detected lead-lag association is a false positive.\nIndividual-method FPR ranges from \\textbf{100\\%} (naive cross-correlation) through 62.5\\% (threshold) and 37.5\\% (differenced) down to \\textbf{0\\%} (permutation Granger: 0/24 across all three seeds).\nThe consensus framework at $K{\\geq}4/5$ achieves \\textbf{FPR${=}$0\\%} (0/24 pairs, three independent seeds), matching the best individual methods while providing robustness against miscalibration of any single method (Table~\\ref{tab:fpr_fnr}).\n\n\\paragraph{False negative rate.}\nAt $\\alpha{=}1.0$, we define 6 effect-size reference pairs (token entropy and ECE $\\to$ distinct-1/2/3) using criteria independent of the consensus methods: drift ratio ${\\geq}3\\times$ the $\\alpha{=}0.0$ baseline (entropy $11.93\\times$, ECE $73.12\\times$) and canary onset strictly preceding downstream onset by ${\\geq}1$ generation (\\S\\ref{sec:calibration}).\nFNR is calibrated on a 4-method panel (excluding differenced cross-correlation to avoid circularity): at $K{\\geq}3/4$, $S{\\geq}3/4$, all~6 are recovered (\\textbf{FNR${=}$0\\%}).\nToda--Yamamoto alone yields FNR${=}$33.3\\% (2/6), missing ECE$\\to$distinct-2 and ECE$\\to$distinct-3 due to Type~II errors at $T{=}10$.\nBoth FPR and FNR are validated on the joint method${\\times}$seed dimension (FPR: 3~seeds at $\\alpha{=}0.0$; FNR: 4~seeds at $\\alpha{=}1.0$), with cross-seed FPR variance negligible (all three seeds yield 0/8 robust pairs at $K{\\geq}4/5$).\nTable~\\ref{tab:fpr_fnr} reports the calibration results.\n\n\\paragraph{Bootstrap power analysis.}\nTo quantify detection power beyond binary FNR, we perform bootstrap power analysis ($B{=}1{,}000$ resamples from 4-seed data at $\\alpha{=}1.0$).\nPermutation Granger achieves power${=}1.0$ on genuine pairs (canary$\\to$diversity) and power${=}0.0$ on spurious pairs (canary$\\to$perplexity), providing clean separation.\nToda--Yamamoto achieves power${=}0.05$--$0.20$ on genuine pairs (depending on $d_{\\max}$), confirming its poor sensitivity at $T{=}10$.\nWe note that bootstrap power${=}1.0$ reflects the effect size in our 4-seed data and should not be interpreted as a universal power guarantee; furthermore, the two methods use different data-generating processes for calibration (permutation Granger resamples observed $\\alpha{=}1.0$ trajectories, while Toda--Yamamoto power was estimated under a generic VAR(2) simulation with $\\beta{=}0.3$).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{False positive and false negative rates for individual methods and the consensus framework (Pythia-410M). The consensus threshold $K{\\geq}4/5$ achieves FPR${=}$0\\% and FNR${=}$0\\%, matching the best individual method (permutation Granger) while providing robustness against miscalibration. FPR: $\\alpha{=}0.0$ null control (three seeds, $M{=}5$ methods); values are 3-seed means with ranges in parentheses where cross-seed variance is non-negligible. FNR: $\\alpha{=}1.0$ against 6 effect-size reference pairs (4-method panel excluding differenced cross-correlation, 4~seeds).}\n\\label{tab:fpr_fnr}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nCriterion & FPR ($\\alpha{=}0.0$) & FNR ($\\alpha{=}1.0$) \\\\\n\\midrule\nNaive XCorr              & 100\\%              & 0\\%  \\\\\nDifferenced XCorr        & 37.5\\%             & 0\\%  \\\\\nThreshold Onset          & 29.2\\% (12.5--62.5) & 0\\%  \\\\\nPermutation Granger      & 0\\%                & 0\\%  \\\\\nToda--Yamamoto           & 4.2\\% (0--12.5)    & 33.3\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nConsensus ($K{\\geq}4/5$)              & 0\\%    & 0\\%  \\\\\nConsensus ($K{\\geq}4/5$, $S{\\geq}3/4$) & 0\\%    & 0\\%  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\nPAPER_EOF",
